(Co)Minuscule Hecke categories
J. Baine

Abstract
We determine the p p -Kazhdan-Lusztig bases for antispherical (co)minuscule Hecke categories in all characteristics, and for spherical (co)minuscule Hecke categories in good characteristic. This is achieved using geometric and diagrammatic methods. The 2 2 -Kazhdan-Lusztig bases of antispherical cominuscule Hecke categories exhibit extremely pathological behaviour. The notions of p p -small resolutions and p p -tight elements are introduced and conjecturally explain this behaviour.



1
Introduction

1.1
Parabolic
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig theory

Categories whose Grothendieck groups are isomorphic to Hecke algebras are ubiquitous in mathematics.
They arise in geometry as perverse sheaves on flag varieties
[
KL2
]
, in Lie theory as blocks of BGG category
\mathcal{O}

[
BGG
]
, and in algebra as Soergel bimodules
[
Soe2
]
.
Such categories are known as Hecke categories.
Kazhdan-Lusztig theory encodes deep, structural properties of Hecke categories in Kazhdan-Lusztig bases of the corresponding Hecke algebra
[
KL1
]
.

Deodhar
[
Deo1
]
introduced parabolic Kazhdan-Lusztig theory, where one instead considers modules for the Hecke algebra obtained by inducing the trivial or sign representations of parabolic subalgebras; these are known as spherical and antispherical modules respectively.
For Weyl groups, these modules are categorified by certain classes of ‘mixed’ sheaves on the corresponding partial flag variety.
The category of parity sheaves is known as the spherical category as it categorifies the spherical module; the category of mixed tilting sheaves is known as the antispherical Hecke category as it categorifies the antispherical module.

When these categories are
\Bbbk
-linear over a field of characteristic 0, the indecomposable sheaves categorify the Kazhdan-Lusztig bases of their respective modules.
If
\Bbbk
is a field of characteristic
p>0
, the indecomposable sheaves give rise to the celebrated
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases of their respective modules.

p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases are fundamental objects in modular representation theory; they encode the characters of simple and tilting modules for algebraic groups
[
AMRW
,
RW2
]
and the decomposition numbers of simple modules for symmetric groups
[
Erd
]
.

Despite their wide-ranging importance, categories with well-understood Kazhdan-Lusztig theory are rare.
Categories with well-understood
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig theory are rarer.
At present, we only ‘understand’ the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig theory arising from dihedral groups, the closely related universal Coxeter groups
[
JW
,
EL
]
, and Hermitian symmetric pairs
[
BDHN
]
.
In this paper we determine the parabolic
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases arising from (co)minuscule partial flag varieties.

1.2
Main results

(Co)Minuscule partial flag varieties are among the best understood projective varieties.
They include projective spaces, (isotropic) Grassmannians, quadric hypersurfaces and certain exceptional partial flag varieties.
A complete list is given in Table
1
.
We call a category a (co)minuscule Hecke category if it is equivalent to either the category of parity sheaves or the category of mixed tilting sheaves on a (co)minuscule partial flag variety.

The first main result of this paper is the determination of the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases of antispherical cominuscule Hecke categories.

Theorem 1.1
.

For any antispherical cominuscule Hecke category the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis is the: Bott-Samelson basis when
p=2
; and, the Kazhdan-Lusztig basis when
p\neq 2
.

The Bott-Samelson basis is introduced in Section
2.2
; it is the crudest upper-bound for the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis.
Theorem
1.1
can be understood as saying the
2
-Kazhdan-Lusztig theory of antispherical cominuscule Hecke categories is as complicated as it can be, whilst the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig theory (when
p\neq 2
) is as simple as it can be.
The dramatic jump in complexity is illustrated for the Lagrangian Grassmannian in Figures
1
and
2
.
1
1

1

See Section
11.2
for details on how to interpret Figures
1
and
2
.

We provide three proofs of Theorem
1.1
.
When
p>2
, we provide a geometric proof using modular Koszul duality in Section
5.2
.
The second proof, which holds for all
p
, explicitly calculates the relevant local intersection forms.
The third, which holds for all
p
, explicitly calculates the relevant idempotents projecting from the Bott-Samelson object onto the indecomposable object in the diagrammatic antispherical category.

In Section
10.1
, we introduce the geometric notion of
p
-small resolutions of singularities, which generalises the classical notion of small resolutions.
We conjecture a geometric explanation for the characteristic 2 phenomena in terms of
2
-small resolutions of Schubert varieties in the Langlands dual, full flag variety.
A related notion of
p
-tight elements is also introduced.
We suspect these notions will be of independent interest.

We also determine the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases of antispherical minuscule Hecke categories.

Theorem 1.2
.

For any antispherical minuscule Hecke category the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis is the Kazhdan-Lusztig basis for all
p
.

This re-proves the main result of
[
BDHN
]
.
Again, we give three proofs: one geometric, one based on local intersections forms, and one by explicitly calculating idempotents.
The geometric proof in Section
5.1
is extremely short, utilising Koszul duality and certain classically known isomorphisms of minuscule partial flag varieties.

Classical antispherical Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials for (co)minuscule partial flag varieties satisfy the remarkable ‘monomial property:’ they are always either
0
or a monic monomial
[
Bre1
,
Bre2
]
.
It follows immediately from Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
and
[
BDF+
]
that this property continues to hold in the modular setting.

Theorem 1.3
.

For any antispherical (co)minuscule Hecke category and any
p\geq 0
, every
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomial is either 0 or a monic monomial.

Indeed, Theorem
1.1
shows that the monomial property is really a property of the Bott-Samelson basis that is inherited by the (
p
-)Kazhdan-Lusztig basis.
A geometric consequence of this property is each indecomposable Whittaker parity sheaf in a (co)minuscule Hecke category is perverse.

The final main result of this work is the determination of the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis of any spherical (co)minuscule Hecke category in good characteristic.

Theorem 1.4
.

For any spherical (co)minuscule Hecke category the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis is the Kazhdan-Lusztig basis when
p
is a good prime.

This is shown using using categorical arguments.
In particular, we construct mixed, parabolic Ringel duality functors in Section
8.2
.
We then related the graded decomposition numbers of mixed tilting, projective and intersection cohomology sheaves, and parity sheaves on the Langlands dual partial flag variety, generalising
[
AR1
, §2.7]
.
Finally we show that, under mild conditions on
p
, if the antispherical
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis is the antispherical Kazhdan-Lusztig basis, then the spherical
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis is the spherical Kazhdan-Lusztig basis.
We expect the results of this section will be of independent interest.

:
Antispherical Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials for the Lagrangian Grassmannian.

Figure 2
:
Antispherical
2
-Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials for the Lagrangian Grassmannian.

1.3
The Lagrangian Grassmannian

The uniformity in the statement of Theorem
1.1
does a disservice to complexity that arises in antispherical cominuscule Hecke categories in characteristic
2
.
The most interesting behaviour occurs when the partial flag variety
G/P
is the Lagrangian Grassmannian.
That is, when
G=Sp_{2n}
and
P
is a parabolic of type
\tA_{n-1}
.

In this case, the indecomposable objects are parameterised by subsets of
x\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
.
To each subset
x
, we associate a combinatorially defined subset
E(x)\subset x
in Section
7.2
.

Proposition 1.5
.

For each
x\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
, the antispherical
2
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis element
{}^{2}d_{x}
decomposes as the following sum of antispherical Kazhdan-Lusztig basis elements
d_{y}
:

\displaystyle{}^{2}d_{x}=\sum_{y\subseteq E(x)}d_{x\backslash y}

where
x\backslash y
denotes the complement of
y
in
x
.

When
x=w_{0}w_{I}
we have
\ell(x)=n(n+1)/2
and
|E(x)|=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
.
Varying
n
gives the first family of elements in finite Weyl groups where the number of non-zero terms in the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis (when expressed in the Kazhdan-Lusztig basis) grows exponentially relative to the length of the element.
The jump in complexity becomes more apparent when one considers the change-of-basis matrices between the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis and the standard basis.
This is depicted in Figures
1
and
2
; see Section
11.2
for further details on interpreting these figures.

In Section
7.2
we explicitly describe all degree-
0
endomorphisms of indecomposable objects in the antispherical category.
Consequently, determine a presentation for the degree-0 endomorphism algebra.

Proposition 1.6
.

If the antispherical Hecke category is
\Bbbk
-linear and
2\notin\Bbbk^{\times}
, then, for any
x\in W^{I}
, the degree-0 endomorphism algebra of the corresponding indecomposable object is

\displaystyle\frac{\Bbbk[\varphi_{E}|E\subseteq E(x)]}{\left(\varphi_{E}\varphi_{E^{\prime}}-2^{|E\cap E^{\prime}|}(-1)^{\ell(E\cap E^{\prime})/2}\varphi_{E\cup E^{\prime}}\right)}.

More generally, for any complete local ring
\Bbbk
, Proposition
1.6
gives a presentation for the degree-0 endomorphism algebra of any Bott-Samelson object indexed by a reduced expression for
x
.
Using this presentation we can explicitly describe the projector onto the maximal indecomposable summand.

Proposition 1.7
.

If
2\in\Bbbk^{\times}
, then for any
x
and any Bott-Samelson indexed by a reduced expression of
x
, the idempotent corresponding to the projector onto the maximal indecomposable summand is given by:

\displaystyle e_{x}=\sum_{E\subseteq E(x)}\frac{(-1)^{\ell(E)/2}}{(-2)^{|E|}}\varphi_{E}=\prod_{t\in E(x)}\left(1-\frac{(-1)^{t/2}}{2}\varphi_{\{t\}}\right).

1.4
Structure

We begin by fixing notation for Coxeter systems and Hecke algebras in Section
2
.
Minuscule and cominuscule partial flag varieties are introduced in Section
3
.
In Section
4
we recall various geometric incarnations of Hecke categories.
The geometric proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
are given in Section
5
.
Diagrammatic Hecke categories are introduced in Section
6
and used to give the remaining proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
in Section
7
.
In Section
8
we study decomposition numbers for various classes of objects under the (derived) extension of scalars and modular reduction functors.
In Section
9
we deduce Theorem
1.4
as an application of the results in Section
8
.
Finally, we introduce the notions of
p
-tight elements and
p
-small resolutions in Section
10
, and conjecture a geometric explanation for the characteristic 2 phenomena occurring in antispherical cominuscule Hecke categories.

2
Coxeter systems and Hecke algebras

2.1
Coxeter systems

We fix notation relating to Coxeter systems.
A standard reference is
[
BB
]
.

Let
(W,S)
be a Coxeter system with length function
\ell
and Bruhat order
\leq
.
We always assume
W
is a finite Weyl group with longest element
w_{0}
.
For any
I\subseteq S
we have the standard
2
2

2

As we exclusively consider standard parabolic subgroups, we will omit the word ‘standard’.
parabolic subgroup
W_{I}
with longest element
w_{I}
.
We denote by
{}^{I}W
(resp.
W^{I}
) the minimal length coset representatives for
W_{I}\backslash W
(resp.
W/W_{I}
).

An
expression
is a sequence
\ux=(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})
where
s_{i}\in S
.
Expressions are always denoted by underlines, i.e.
\ux
.
The length of an expression
\ell(\ux)
is its length as a sequence.
An expression is said to be reduced if
\ell(\ux)=\ell(\ux_{\bullet})
, where
(-)_{\bullet}
is the map into
W
induced by multiplication.
The empty expression
\underline{\emptyset}
is, by definition, a reduced expression for the identity element
\id\in W
.

Later we will consider light-leaves for the antispherical Hecke category.
These are constructed from the data of an
I
-parabolic Bruhat strolls associated to a subexpression.
We recall the following notions from
[
Deo2
,
EW
,
LW
]
.

A
subexpression

\uy\subseteq\ux
is a subsequence
\uy=(s_{i_{1}},\dots,s_{i_{j}})
of
\ux
.
Alternatively, we identify a subexpression
\uy\subseteq\ux
with the decorated sequence
\ux^{e}
where each term is decorated with either 1 (indicating inclusion) or 0 (indicating omission).
When explicitly dealing with subexpressions, we write

\displaystyle\uy=\ux^{e}=\begin{smallmatrix}e_{1}&e_{2}&\dots&e_{k}\\
s_{1}&s_{2}&\dots&s_{k}\end{smallmatrix}.

Each subexpression has an associated
Bruhat stroll
, which is a decoration of
\ux^{e}
defined as follows.
Set
\ux_{0}:=\underline{\emptyset}
,
\ux_{i}:=(s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{i})
,
e_{i}:=(e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{i})
, and
x_{i}:=(\ux_{i}^{e_{i}})_{\bullet}
where
i\leq\ell(\ux)
.
That is,
\ux_{i}
is
\ux^{e}
truncated to the first
i
terms.
The
i
-th term in the Bruhat stroll is defined to be

\displaystyle\ua\text{ if }x_{i-1}s_{i}>x_{i-1}\text{ and,}

\displaystyle\da\text{ if }x_{i-1}s_{i}<x_{i-1}.

The Bruhat stroll of a subexpression will typically be written as

\displaystyle\uy=\ux^{e}=\begin{smallmatrix}\ua&\ua&\da&\dots&\ua\\
0&1&1&\dots&e_{k}\\
s_{1}&s_{2}&s_{3}&\dots&s_{k}\end{smallmatrix}.

A Bruhat stroll is
I
-parabolic
if
x_{i-1}s_{i}\in{}^{I}W
for each
1\leq i\leq\ell(\ux)
.
The
defect

df
of a subexpression
\ux^{e}
is defined as

\displaystyle\text{df}(\ux^{e})=\left(\#\begin{smallmatrix}\ua\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\text{ in the Bruhat stroll of }\ux^{e}\right)-\left(\#\begin{smallmatrix}\da\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\text{ in the Bruhat stroll of }\ux^{e}\right).

Finally, recall that
x\in W
is said to be
fully commutative
, in the sense of
[
Ste
]
, if no reduced expression of
x
contains a consecutive subexpression that is a reduced expression for the longest element of a dihedral group of type
\tI_{2}(m)
where
(m\geq 3)
.

2.2
Hecke algebras

We now fix notation for Hecke algebras.
We use the normalisation in
[
Soe1
]
, and the notation of
[
Wil2
]
.

The Hecke algebra
H
associated to the Coxeter system
(W,S)
is the unital, associative
\Z[v,v^{-1}]
-algebra on the symbols
\{\delta_{x}\,|\,x\in W\}
subject to the relations:

\displaystyle\delta_{x}\delta_{y}=

\displaystyle\delta_{xy}

\displaystyle\text{if }\ell(x)+\ell(y)=\ell(xy)\text{, and}

\displaystyle(\delta_{s}+v)(\delta_{s}-v^{-1})=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\text{for all }s\in S.

It follows from
[
Tits
]
that
\{\delta_{x}\,|\,x\in W\}
is a basis of
H
, known as the standard basis.
For any
I\subseteq S
, we denote by
H_{I}
the corresponding Hecke subalgebra of
H
.

Fix
I\subseteq S
.
Denote by
\triv
(resp.
\sign
) the rank 1
H_{I}
-module where
\delta_{s}
acts by
v^{-1}
(resp.
-v
) for each
s\in I
.
Induction produces the
spherical module

{}^{I}M:=\Ind_{H_{I}}^{H}\triv
and
antispherical module

{}^{I}N:=\Ind_{H_{I}}^{H}\sign
which are right
H
-modules.
Both modules have a standard basis which we abusively denote by
\{\delta_{x}^{I}~|~x\in{}^{I}W\}
, where
\delta_{x}^{I}:=1\otimes\delta_{x}
.

2.3
Kazhdan-Lusztig bases

The Kazhdan-Lusztig involution is the
\Z
-linear involution uniquely characterised by the properties
\overline{v}=v^{-1}
and
\overline{\delta_{x}}=\delta_{x^{-1}}^{-1}
for each
x\in W
.
It induces an involution on
{}^{I}M
and
{}^{I}N
. Each module has
Kazhdan-Lusztig basis

\displaystyle\{b_{x}~|~x\in W\}\subset H,

\displaystyle\{c_{x}~|~x\in{}^{I}W\}\subset{}^{I}M,

\displaystyle\{d_{x}~|~x\in{}^{I}W\}\subset{}^{I}N,

uniquely characterised by being: self-dual
\overline{a_{x}}=a_{x}
; and positively graded
a_{x}\in\delta_{x}+\sum_{y<x}v\Z[v]\delta_{y}
, where
a\in\{b,c,d\}
and
x\in W
or
x\in{}^{I}W
as appropriate,
[
KL1
,
Deo1
]
.
The corresponding
Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials

h_{y,x}
,
m_{y,x}
, and
n_{y,x}
are defined by the equalities:

\displaystyle b_{x}=\sum_{y}h_{y,x}\delta_{y}

\displaystyle c_{x}=\sum_{y}m_{y,x}\delta^{I}_{y}

\displaystyle d_{x}=\sum_{y}n_{y,x}\delta^{I}_{y}.

2.4
Bott-Samelson bases

For any expression
\ux=(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})
define
b_{\ux}:=b_{s_{1}}\dots b_{s_{k}}
.
Similarly define
c_{\ux}=c_{\id}\cdot b_{\ux}
and
d_{\ux}=d_{\id}\cdot b_{\ux}
.
If a reduced expression
\ux
of
x
is fixed for each
x\in W
, then a standard uni-triangularity argument shows

\displaystyle\{b_{\ux}~|~x\in W\}\subset H,

\displaystyle\{c_{\ux}~|~x\in{}^{I}W\}\subset{}^{I}M,

\displaystyle\{d_{\ux}~|~x\in{}^{I}W\}\subset{}^{I}N,

are each bases of their respective modules.
Each will be called a
Bott-Samelson basis
of its module.
Typically the choice of reduced expression is extremely important;
b_{\ux}
is independent of the choice of reduced expression if and only if
x
is fully commutative.
Fortunately, we only ever consider fully commutative elements, so we can safely ignore this subtlety.

Deodhar’s defect formula expresses a Bott-Samelson element in terms of the standard basis
[
Deo2
,
LW
]
.
The versions we require are:

\displaystyle b_{\ux}=\sum_{\ux^{e}\subseteq\ux}v^{\text{df}(\ux^{e})}\delta_{\ux^{e}_{\bullet}}

and

\displaystyle d_{\ux}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ux^{e}\subseteq\ux\\
\ux^{e}\text{is }I\text{-antispherical}\end{subarray}}v^{\text{df}(\ux^{e})}\delta_{\ux^{e}_{\bullet}}^{I}

where
\text{df}(\ux^{e})
denotes the defect of the subexpression.

2.5
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases

The
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases were introduced in
[
Wil1
,
JW
,
RW1
]
.
Unlike the preceding bases they are not intrinsic to the Hecke algebra.
Rather, the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases

\displaystyle\{{}^{p}b_{x}~|~x\in W\}\subset H,

\displaystyle\{{}^{p}c_{x}~|~x\in{}^{I}W\}\subset{}^{I}M,

\displaystyle\{{}^{p}d_{x}~|~x\in{}^{I}W\}\subset{}^{I}N,

arise as the images under the character map of the indecomposable parity sheaves in the Hecke categories introduced in Section
4
.
The corresponding
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials

{}^{p}h_{y,x}
,
{}^{p}m_{y,x}
, and
{}^{p}n_{y,x}
are defined by the equalities:

\displaystyle{}^{p}b_{x}=\sum_{y}{}^{p}h_{y,x}\delta_{y}

\displaystyle{}^{p}c_{x}=\sum_{y}{}^{p}m_{y,x}\delta^{I}_{y}

\displaystyle{}^{p}d_{x}=\sum_{y}{}^{p}n_{y,x}\delta^{I}_{y}.

The following basic properties of
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials are shown in
[
JW
, §4]
.
Let
x\in W
be arbitrary, and fix a reduced expression
\ux
of
x
, then

(a)

we have
{}^{p}b_{x}\in b_{x}+\sum_{y<x}\Z_{\geq 0}[v,v^{-1}]b_{y}
, and

(b)

we have
b_{\ux}\in{}^{p}b_{x}+\sum_{y<x}\Z_{\geq 0}[v,v^{-1}]{}^{p}b_{y}
.

Analogous statements hold for
{}^{p}c_{x}
and
{}^{p}d_{x}
.
These allow us to obtain crude bounds for
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis purely using the Hecke algebra.
For any two
h,h^{\prime}\in H
, we write
h\preceq h^{\prime}
if
h^{\prime}-h\in\sum_{y}\Z_{\geq 0}[v,v^{-1}]b_{y}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

For any
x\in W
and any reduced expression
\ux
of
x
, we have
b_{x}\preceq{}^{p}b_{x}\preceq b_{\ux}
for all
p
.
In particular, if
b_{x}=b_{\ux}
then
b_{x}={}^{p}b_{x}=b_{\ux}
.

Proof.

This is immediate from properties (a) and (b) listed above.
∎

The obvious analogue of Lemma
2.1
holds for
{}^{p}c_{x}
and
{}^{p}d_{x}
.

3
(Co)Minuscule partial flag varieties

We begin by fixing notation for algebraic groups, then we recall the definition of (co)minuscule partial flag varieties and their classification.
Finally we note various properties of these varieties that will be exploited in later sections.

For a thorough account of the geometry of (co)minuscule partial flag varieties see
[
BL
, §9]
.

3.1
Algebraic groups and flag varieties

Fix a simple root datum
(R\subset X,\check{R}\subset\check{X})
and a set of positive roots
R_{+}\subset R
.
Associated to this data is the Chevalley group scheme
G_{\Z}
, Borel subgroup scheme
B_{\Z}
and maximal torus subgroup scheme
T_{\Z}
.
Let
U_{\Z}\subset B_{\Z}
be the unipotent radical subgroup scheme of
B_{\Z}
.
The choice of positive roots endows the Weyl group
W
of
G
with a set of simple reflections.
For any
I\subseteq S
, there is an associated parabolic subgroup
P_{I,\Z}
.
When
I
is clear, we write
P_{\Z}
.
Denote by
G
,
B
,
T
,
U
,
P
the corresponding schemes over
\C
obtained via extension of scalars.
By our assumptions,
G
is a simple algebraic group.

Given
G
, the Langlands dual group
\check{G}
is the group associated to the root datum
(\check{R}\subset\check{X},R\subset X)
.
We denote the corresponding subgroup schemes of
\check{G}
by
\check{B}
,
\check{T}
,
\check{U}
,
\check{P}
, etc.

Finally, the partial flag variety
G/P
admits a Bruhat stratification, i.e. a stratification
B
-orbits:

\displaystyle G/P=\bigsqcup_{x\in W^{I}}B\cdot xP/P.

The closure of each strata
X_{x}:=\overline{B\cdot xP/P}=\sqcup_{y\leq x}B\cdot yP/P
is a Schubert variety.

3.2
Classification of (co)minuscule flag varieties

We now recall the classification of (co)minuscule flag varieties.

Fix a simple algebraic group
G
.
A fundamental weight
\varpi\in X
for
G
is said to be:
minuscule
if
\left\langle\alphac,\varpi\right\rangle\leq 1
for all
\alpha\in R_{+}
; and
cominuscule
if
\left\langle\alphac_{0},\varpi\right\rangle=1
, where
\alpha_{0}
is the longest root.
Various equivalent definitions are noted in
[
BL
, §2.11.15]
.
A maximal parabolic subgroup
P_{I}
is said to be
(co)minuscule
when
I=S\backslash\{s_{\varpi}\}
, where
s_{\varpi}
is the simple reflection corresponding to a (co)minuscule weight.
The variety
G/P_{I}
is a
(co)minuscule flag variety
if
P_{I}
is a (co)minuscule parabolic subgroup.
Note that minuscule and cominuscule flag varieties are related by Langlands duality: if
G/P
is minuscule then
\check{G}/\check{P}
is cominuscule and vice versa.

(Co)Minuscule partial flag varieties arise from seven families and two exceptional cases, which are listed in Table
1
.

G

P

Classification

Description

A_{n}

Both

Grassmannian

B_{n}

minuscule

Odd orthogonal Grassmannian

B_{n}

cominuscule

Odd dimensional quadric

C_{n\geq 3}

cominuscule

Lagrangian Grassmannian

C_{n\geq 3}

minuscule

Projective space

Even dimensional quadric

E_{6}

Minuscule and cominuscule partial flag varieties.

3.3
Non-simply laced minuscule flag varieties.

It is well-known that when studying Schubert varieties in minuscule flag varieties, it suffices to consider only the those Schubert varieties arising from simply-laced minuscule flag varieties (i.e. when
G
is simply-laced) by the following Lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

For any non-simply-laced minuscule flag variety
G/P
, there is a simply laced minuscule flag variety
G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}
and an isomorphism
f:G/P\rightarrow G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}
, which respects the Bruhat stratification of each partial flag variety.

Proof.

This is
[
BP
, §3.1]
.
∎

These isomorphisms are quite explicit.
When
G
is type
\tC_{n}
and
P
is type
\tC_{n-1}
, the variety
G/P
is complex projective space
\bP^{2n-1}
and the corresponding
G^{\prime}
is always type
\tA_{2n-1}
with
P^{\prime}
type
\tA_{2n-2}
.
When
G
is type
\tB_{n}
and
P
is type
\tA_{n-1}
, the variety
G/P
is the odd orthogonal Grassmannian, which can be identified with the spinor variety, and the corresponding
G^{\prime}
is always type
\tD_{n+1}
with
P^{\prime}
type
\tA_{n}
.

By an abuse of notation we write
f:W^{I}\longrightarrow W^{\prime I^{\prime}}
for the map induced on minimal coset representatives; it is described in
[
BM
]
.

4
Geometric Hecke categories

We now introduce various geometric categories which categorify either the spherical or antispherical module.
Our treatment will be quite terse; the interested reader should consult
[
JMW
,
AR1
,
AR2
,
RW1
,
AMRW
]
for more details.

4.1
Parity sheaves and the spherical Hecke category

As noted in the introduction, the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis of the spherical module
M^{I}
will be categorified by parity sheaves on the partial flag variety
G/P_{I}
.

We continue the notation for algebraic groups from Section
3.1
. For any of the categories of sheaves considered below, when the ring of coefficients
\Bbbk
is clear, we omit it from the notation.

Fix
\Bbbk
, either a complete local ring or a field. We denote by

\displaystyle D^{b}(U\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)

the bounded, derived category of sheaves of
\Bbbk
-modules on
G/P
which are constructible with respect to the stratification by
B
-orbits.
It is triangulated, and we denote the shift functor by
(1)
.
The ‘stacky’ notation is motivated by the following equivalences which are explained in
[
AR1
, §5.3]
:
(a) the forgetful functor from the
U
-equivariant derived category of constructible sheaves of
\Bbbk
-modules on
G/P
to
D^{b}(U\backslash G/P)
is an equivalence; and
(b) the aforementioned
U
-equivariant category is naturally equivalent to the
P^{\text{op}}
-equivariant derived category of constructible sheaves of
\Bbbk
-modules on
U\backslash G
, where the action is induced by right multiplication.

Parity sheaves were introduced in
[
JMW
]
, they are certain classes of sheaves satisfying cohomological parity vanishing properties; stalks and costalks of indecomposable parity sheaves are concentrated in either even or odd degrees.
We denote by

\displaystyle\Parity(U\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)

the full subcategory of
parity sheaves
in
D^{b}(U\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)
.
It is stable under the shift functor
(1)
.
Since we assume
\Bbbk
is a complete local ring,
\Parity(U\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)
is Krull-Schmidt, and isomorphism classes of indecomposable parity sheaves
\cE_{x}(n)
are parameterised by
(x,n)\in W^{I}\times\Z
.
We write
\cE_{x}
for
\cE_{x}(0)
.
Each
\cE_{x}
is characterised by the properties:

(a)

\cE_{x}
is supported on the Schubert variety
X_{x}
;

(b)

\cE_{x}
restricted to
B\cdot xP/P
is isomorphic to
\underline{\Bbbk}_{B\cdot xP/P}(\ell(x))
; and

(c)

the stalks and costalks of
\cE_{x}
vanish in degree
d
where
d\not\equiv\ell(x)\mod 2
.

We note, but will not use, that
\Parity(U\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)
is a right-module category over the analogously defined
\Parity(P\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)
, where the monoidal action is induced by convolution of sheaves.

The category
\Parity(U\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)
categorifies the spherical module.
3
3

3

More accurately
\Parity(B\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)
categorifies the spherical
H
-module
M^{I}
, as convolution endows
\Parity(B\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)
with the structure of a left-module category over
\Parity(B\backslash G/B,\Bbbk)
. Then
\Parity(U\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)
categorifies the
\Z[v,v^{-1}]
-module obtained by forgetting the left
H
-action on
M^{I}
.

In particular, we have an isomorphism of
\Z[v,v^{-1}]
-modules:

\ch:

[\Parity(U\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)]

\tilde{\longrightarrow}

M^{I}

[\mathcal{F}]

\longmapsto

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in W^{I}}\sum_{i\in\Z}\rank_{\Bbbk}H^{i}(\mathcal{F}_{xP/P})v^{-\ell(x)-i}\delta_{x}^{I}

where
\rank_{\Bbbk}H^{i}(\mathcal{F}_{xP/P})
denotes the rank of the cohomology of the stalk of
\mathcal{F}
at the point
xP/P
.
Note that if
\Bbbk
is a field of characteristic
p
or a complete local ring with residue field of characteristic
p
, then, by definition, we have
\ch[\cE_{x}]={}^{p}c_{x}
.
When
I=\emptyset
then
M^{I}\cong H
and
\ch[\cE_{x}]={}^{p}b_{x}
.
Finally, if
p=0
then indecomposable parity sheaves are simple intersection cohomology sheaves, so we obtain the classical Kazhdan-Lusztig basis by
[
KL2
,
Deo1
]
.

4.2
Whittaker sheaves and the antispherical Hecke category

Fix an algebraically closed field
\mathbb{L}
of characteristic
\ell\neq p
such that there is a non-trivial additive character
\psi:\Z/\ell\Z\rightarrow\Bbbk^{\times}
.
In a deviation from the notation in Section
3.1
, we have group schemes
G
,
B
,
T
,
U
, and
P_{I}
, but they are now considered as schemes over
\mathbb{L}
via extension of scalars.
In this context,
D^{b}(H\backslash G/K,\Bbbk)
now denotes the analogously defined category of sheaves in the étale topology.

Whittaker sheaves arise from an alternate stratification of the
full
flag variety
B\backslash G
.
We first need to introduce two more groups:
U^{I}
and
U_{I}^{-}
.
The group
U^{I}
is the unipotent radical of
P_{I}
.
If
L_{I}\subset P_{I}
denotes the Levi factor, then
U_{I}^{-}
is defined to be the unipotent radical of the Borel subgroup of
L_{I}
that is opposite
L_{I}\cap B
.
The desired stratification of
B\backslash G
is by
U_{I}^{-}U^{I}
-orbits:

\displaystyle B\backslash G=\bigsqcup_{x\in W}B\backslash Bx\cdot U_{I}^{-}U^{I}.

When
x\in W^{I}
, the stratum
B\backslash Bx\cdot U_{I}^{-}U^{I}
has dimension
\ell(xw_{I})
.

If we fix an isomorphism
U_{s}^{-}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{a}
for each
s\in I
, then we obtain a morphism of algebraic groups:

\displaystyle\chi:U_{I}^{-}U^{I}\longrightarrow U_{I}^{-}\longrightarrow U_{I}^{-}/[U_{I}^{-},U_{I}^{-}]\tilde{\longrightarrow}\prod_{s\in I}U_{s}^{-}\tilde{\longrightarrow}(\mathbb{G}_{a})^{I}\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{a}

where the third map is the inverse of the natural embedding, and the final map is induced by addition.
Fix a non-trivial additive character
\psi:\Z/\ell\Z\rightarrow\Bbbk^{\times}
.
The Artin-Schreier sheaf
\mathcal{L}_{\psi}
is the rank-one local system on
\mathbb{G}_{a}
which arises as the
\psi
-isotypic component of
\alpha_{*}\underline{\Bbbk}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}}
, where
\alpha:\mathbb{G}_{a}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{a}
is the Artin-Schreier map
\alpha(x)=x^{\ell}-x
.
The sheaf
\chi_{I}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\psi}
is multiplicative, in the sense of
[
AR1
, §A.1]
, so one can consider twisted
(U^{I}U_{I}^{-},\chi_{I}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\psi})
-equivariant complexes on
B\backslash G
.

The triangulated category of twisted
(U_{I}^{-}U^{I},\chi_{I}^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\psi})
-equivariant complexes on
B\backslash G
is denoted by

\displaystyle D_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G,\Bbbk).

The definition of parity objects carries over to
D_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G,\Bbbk)
, and we denote by

\displaystyle\Parity_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G,\Bbbk)

the full subcategory of parity objects in
D_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)
.
Since
\Bbbk
is a complete local ring or a field, the category
\Parity_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)
is Krull-Schmidt and isomorphism classes of indecomposable parity sheaves
\cE_{x}(n)
are parameterised by
(x,n)\in W^{I}\times\Z
and admit an analogous characterisation to that in Section
4.1
.

The category
\Parity_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)
categorifies the antispherical module
N^{I}
.
4
4

4

\Parity_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)
can be endowed with the structure of a left-module category over
\Parity(B\backslash G/B)
where the action is via convolution; see
[
RW1
, §11.1]
.

In particular, there is an isomorphism of
H
-modules given by

\ch:

[\Parity_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G,\Bbbk)]

\tilde{\longrightarrow}

N^{I}

[\mathcal{F}]

\longmapsto

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in W}\sum_{i\in\Z}\rank_{\Bbbk}H^{i}(\mathcal{F}_{B\backslash Bx})v^{-\ell(x)-i}\delta_{x}^{I}

where
\rank_{\Bbbk}H^{i}(\mathcal{F}_{B\backslash Bx})
denotes the rank of the cohomology of the stalk of
\mathcal{F}
at the point
B\backslash Bx
.
If
\Bbbk
is a field of characteristic
p
or a complete local ring with residue field of characteristic
p
, then, by definition, we have
\ch[\cE_{x}]={}^{p}d_{x}
.
When
p=0
we have
\ch[\cE_{x}]=d_{x}
by
[
AB
]
.

4.3
A quotient construction of antispherical Hecke categories

The antispherical module
N^{I}
can be constructed as a quotient of the Hecke algebra

\displaystyle N^{I}\cong H~/~(b_{z}~|~z\notin W^{I}),

where the image of
b_{x}
identifies with
d_{x}
when
x\in W^{I}
, see
[
LW
, §1.3]
.
Completely analogously, we can consider the category

\displaystyle\Parity(B\backslash G/U)~/~\langle\cE_{x}~|~x\notin W^{I}\rangle_{\oplus,(1)}

where
\langle\cE_{x}\,|\,x\notin W^{I}\rangle_{\oplus,(1)}
denotes the subcategory of
\Parity(B\backslash G/U)
additively generated by the
\cE_{x}
, and closed under the shift functor
(1)
.
Morphisms in this category are morphisms in
\Parity(B\backslash G/U)
modulo those factoring through any object in
\langle\cE_{x}\,|\,x\notin W^{I}\rangle_{\oplus,(1)}
.
By
[
RW1
, §11.5]
and
[
AMRW
, §6.2]
we have an equivalence

\displaystyle Av:\Parity(B\backslash G/U)~/~\langle\cE_{x}~|~x\notin W^{I}\rangle_{\oplus,(1)}\longrightarrow\Parity_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G,\Bbbk)

satisfying
Av(\cE_{x})\cong\cE_{x}
for each
x\in W
.

4.4
Mixed Hecke categories

In this Section we recall mixed Hecke categories.
These are modular analogues of the category of mixed
\Q_{p}
-sheaves on flag varieties that were studied in
[
BGS
]
.
This approach to mixed modular sheaves stems from
[
AR1
,
AMRW
]
. One of the main reasons for considering these categories is that they are related by Koszul duality.

The
mixed derived spherical Hecke category

D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)
is defined as

\displaystyle D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)

\displaystyle:=K^{b}(\Parity(U\backslash G/P)).

Similarly, the
mixed derived antispherical Hecke category

D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(G/B)
is defined as

\displaystyle D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)

\displaystyle:=K^{b}(\Parity_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G))

where, in both cases,
K^{b}
denotes the bounded homotopy category.
Each category is endowed with an internal shift functor
(1)
and a cohomological shift functor
[1]
.
We define the tate twist
\langle 1\rangle
as
(-1)[1]
.

Each of the mixed Hecke categories admits a canonical perverse
t
-structure.
We will not require the precise definition, instead referring the interested reader to
[
AR2
, §3]
and
[
AMRW
, §6]
in the case of mixed spherical Hecke categories, and
[
AMRW
, §6]
for mixed antispherical Hecke categories.
The
mixed perverse categories
, denoted

\displaystyle\Pmix(U\backslash G/P)

\displaystyle\Pmixw(B\backslash G),

arise as the hearts of the perverse
t
-structures on
D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)
and
D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)
respectively.

We now write
\Pmix
to mean either of the mixed perverse Hecke categories introduced above, as each of the following statements applies to each category.
The tate twist
\langle 1\rangle
is
t
-exact.
The category
\Pmix
has the structure of a graded highest weight category, in the sense of
[
AR2
, §A]
,
5
5

5

Where they are instead called quasihereditary.
where
\langle 1\rangle
is the shift functor.
The graded highest weight structure implies isomorphism classes of indecomposable tilting
T_{x}
, standard
\Delta_{x}
, costandard
\nabla_{x}
, and simple
L_{x}
objects are parameterised (up to shift) by
x\in W^{I}
.
Since
W
is finite
\Pmix
has enough projectives; we denote a projective cover of
L_{x}
by
P_{x}
.
We denote by

\displaystyle\Tilt^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)

\displaystyle\Tilt_{Wh,I}^{\mix}(B\backslash G)

the full subcategories of tilting objects in the relevant perverse Hecke category.

Suppose
\Bbbk
is a field or complete local ring.
Modular
Koszul duality

[
AMRW
,
RV
]
establishes an equivalence of triangulated categories

\displaystyle\kappa:D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/B)~~\tilde{\longrightarrow}~~D^{\mix}(\check{B}\backslash\check{G}/\check{U})

satisfying
\kappa\circ\langle 1\rangle\cong(1)\circ\kappa
and

\displaystyle\kappa(T_{x})\cong\check{\cE}_{x},

\displaystyle\kappa(\cE_{x})\cong\check{T}_{x},

\displaystyle\kappa(\Delta_{x})\cong\check{\Delta}_{x}

\displaystyle\kappa(\nabla_{x})\cong\check{\nabla}_{x}.

Note that the groups
\check{G},\check{B}
and
\check{U}
are the Langlands dual groups to
G,B
and
U
respectively.
Parabolic Koszul duality was also considered in
[
AMRW
]
, where it was shown that if
2\in\Bbbk^{\times}
, there is an equivalence of triangulated categories

\displaystyle\kappa:D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)~~\tilde{\longrightarrow}~~D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(\check{B}\backslash\check{G})

again satisfying
\kappa\circ\langle 1\rangle\cong(1)\circ\kappa
and

\displaystyle\kappa(T_{x})\cong\check{\cE}_{x},

\displaystyle\kappa(\cE_{x})\cong\check{T}_{x},

\displaystyle\kappa(\Delta_{x})\cong\check{\Delta}_{x},

\displaystyle\kappa(\nabla_{x})\cong\check{\nabla}_{x}.

We also denote the map induced on Grothendieck groups by
\kappa
.

5
Geometric antispherical (co)minuscule Hecke categories

If
\Bbbk
is a field or complete local ring such that
2\in\Bbbk^{\times}
, parabolic Koszul duality induces an equivalence

\displaystyle\kappa:\Tilt^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)~~\tilde{\longrightarrow}~~\Parity_{Wh,I}(\check{B}\backslash\check{G},\Bbbk).

For this reason, we call
\Parity_{Wh,I}(\check{B}\backslash\check{G},\Bbbk)
an antispherical (co)minuscule Hecke category when
G/P
is a (co)minuscule flag variety.
We continue nomenclature even when
2\notin\Bbbk
.

We now prove, using geometric techniques, that the antispherical
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis for antispherical (co)minuscule Hecke categories is the Kazhdan-Lusztig basis whenever
p>2
.

5.1
Antispherical minuscule Hecke categories

We begin by showing that we can reduce to the simply-laced case.
Throughout this section we always take
\Bbbk
to be a field or a complete local ring.

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose
2\in\Bbbk^{\times}
.
For any non-simply-laced
\check{G}
such that
\Parity_{Wh,I}(\check{B}\backslash\check{G},\Bbbk)
is minuscule, there is a simply-laced
\check{G}^{\prime}
such that
\Parity_{Wh,I^{\prime}}(\check{B}^{\prime}\backslash\check{G}^{\prime},\Bbbk)
is minuscule and an equivalence

\displaystyle\check{f}_{*}:\Parity_{Wh,I}(\check{B}\backslash\check{G},\Bbbk)~~\longrightarrow~~\Parity_{Wh,I^{\prime}}(\check{B}^{\prime}\backslash\check{G}^{\prime},\Bbbk)

satisfying
\check{f}_{*}(\cE_{x})\cong\cE_{f(x)}^{\prime}
for each
x\in W^{I}
.

Proof.

By assumption on
\check{G}
and Lemma
3.1
, there is a simply-laced
G^{\prime}
, a minuscule flag variety
G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}
, and isomorphism
f:G/P\rightarrow G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}
satisfying
B\cdot xP/P\cong B^{\prime}\cdot f(x)P^{\prime}/P^{\prime}
for each
x\in W^{I}
.
Hence
f
is a stratified isomorphism, in the sense of
[
JMW
, §2.4]
, which induces an equivalence of (constructible) derived categories

\displaystyle f_{*}:D_{(B)}^{b}(G/P)~~\longrightarrow~~D_{(B^{\prime})}^{b}(G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}).

After identifying
D^{b}(U\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)
with
D_{(B)}^{b}(G/P,\Bbbk)
via the forgetful functor, this restricts to an equivalence

\displaystyle f_{*}:\Parity(U\backslash G/P)~~\longrightarrow~~\Parity(U^{\prime}\backslash G^{\prime}/P^{\prime}).

The equivalence is extremely naive: the identification of
B\cdot xP/P
with
B^{\prime}\cdot f(x)P^{\prime}/P^{\prime}
induces an isomorphism
f_{*}(\cE_{x})\cong\cE_{f(x)}^{\prime}
.
Passing to bounded homotopy categories, and then restricting to the full subcategories of tilting sheaves gives an equivalence

\displaystyle f_{*}:\Tilt^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)~~\longrightarrow~~\Tilt^{\mix}(U^{\prime}\backslash G^{\prime}/P^{\prime})

satisfying
f_{*}(T_{x})\cong T_{f(x)}^{\prime}
.
The desired functor is then obtained as the composition

\displaystyle\check{f}_{*}:\Parity_{Wh,I}(\check{B}\backslash\check{G})~\xrightarrow{\kappa}~\Tilt^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)~\xrightarrow{f_{*}}~\Tilt^{\mix}(U^{\prime}\backslash G^{\prime}/P^{\prime})~\xrightarrow{\kappa^{\prime}}~\Parity_{Wh,I}(\check{B}^{\prime}\backslash\check{G}^{\prime}).

which evidently satisfies
\check{f}_{*}(\cE_{x})\cong\cE_{f(x)}^{\prime}
.
∎

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
2\in\Bbbk^{\times}
and
\Parity_{Wh,I}(\check{B}\backslash\check{G},\Bbbk)
be an antispherical minuscule Hecke category.
Then
{}^{p}d_{x}=d_{x}
for all
x\in W^{I}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.1
, it suffices to consider only the simply-laced cases.
However, in each simply-laced case it is known that for each reduced expression
\ux
of
x\in W^{I}
we have
d_{x}=d_{\ux}
, so we must have
d_{x}={}^{p}d_{x}=d_{\ux}
by Lemma
2.1
.
For proofs of
d_{x}=d_{\ux}
, see
[
Bre1
, Theorem 5.1]
for Grassmannians and
[
Bre2
, Theorem 4.1]
for Spinor varieties and even dimensional quadrics, the exceptional cases are easily checked by hand.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

The condition
p>2
in Lemma
5.1
is a consequence of a technical difficulty in the construction of Koszul duality (and consequently parabolic Koszul duality) in
[
AMRW
]
.
In
[
RV
]
Riche and Vay overcome this technical condition, constructing Koszul duality for all
p
.
However, they do not consider parabolic Koszul duality.
If parabolic Koszul duality is constructed for all
p
, the geometric proof of Theorem
5.2
will hold for all
p
.

5.2
Antispherical cominuscule Hecke categories

We now determine the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases for antispherical cominuscule Hecke categories.
By Theorem
5.2
, it suffices to consider only the non-simply-laced cases, which are the Lagrangian Grassmannian and odd dimensional quadrics.

First, recall that if
G
is a simple algebraic group with root system
R
, then
p
is a good prime for
G
if
p
does not divide any coefficient of the highest root
\alpha\in R
when expressed as a sum of simple roots.
A prime
p
is said to be very good if either:
G
is not type
\tA
and
p
is good; or
G
is type
\tA_{n}
and
p\not|n+1
.
See Table
2
for (very) good primes in each type.

Type

\tA_{n}

\tB_{n}
,
\tC_{n}
or
\tD_{n}

\textbf{G}_{2}
,
\tF_{4}
,
\tE_{6}
or
\tE_{7}

\tE_{8}

Good primes

p>0

p>2

p>3

p>5

Very good primes

p~\not|~n+1

p>2

p>3

p>5

Table 2
:
Good and very good primes for simple algebraic groups.

We note the following equivalence, from which we will deduce our result.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
G
be a simply-connected, simple algebraic group, and
p\in\Bbbk^{\times}
for each
p
which is not a very good prime for
G
.
Then there is an equivalence of triangulated categories

\displaystyle\varkappa:D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/B)\longrightarrow D^{\mix}(\check{U}\backslash\check{G}/\check{B})

satisfying
\varkappa(\cE_{x})\cong\check{\cE}_{x}
for all
x\in W
.

Proof.

The desired equivalence is obtained by composing the two forms of Koszul duality constructed in
[
AMRW
]
.
Namely, those in Theorems 5.6 and 7.2.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
G
be simply-connected and non-simply-laced,
\Parity_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)
cominuscule, and
2\in\Bbbk^{\times}
.
There is an equivalence

\displaystyle\varkappa^{Av}:\Parity_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G,\Bbbk)\longrightarrow\Parity_{Wh,I}(\check{B}\backslash\check{G},\Bbbk)

satisfying
\varkappa^{Av}(\cE_{x})\cong\cE_{x}
for each
x\in W
.

Proof.

By the classification of non-simply-laced cominuscule flag varieties, it suffices to ensure
2\in\Bbbk^{\times}
to apply Lemma
5.4
.
Observe that
\varkappa
restricts to an equivalence of the graded, additive subcategories

\displaystyle\varkappa:\langle\cE_{x}~|~x\notin W^{I}\rangle_{\oplus,(1)}\longrightarrow\langle\check{\cE}_{x}~|~x\notin W^{I}\rangle_{\oplus,(1)}

Hence
\varkappa
induces an equivalence of the quotient categories

\displaystyle\varkappa:\Parity(U\backslash G/B)~/~\langle\cE_{x}~|~x\notin W^{I}\rangle_{\oplus,(1)}\longrightarrow\Parity(\check{U}\backslash\check{G}/\check{B})~/~\langle\check{\cE}_{x}~|~x\notin W^{I}\rangle_{\oplus,(1)}.

Now recall the equivalence
Av
from Section
4.3
, and denote by
Av^{-1}
the quasi-inverse functor.
The composition
\check{A}v^{-1}\circ\varkappa\circ Av
gives the desired equivalence
\varkappa^{Av}
, it is easy to check it satisfies
\varkappa^{Av}(\cE_{x})\cong\cE_{x}
for all
x\in W
.
∎

We can now deduce the main result of this Section.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
2\in\Bbbk^{\times}
and
\Parity_{Wh,I}(\check{B}\backslash\check{G},\Bbbk)
be an antispherical cominuscule Hecke category.
Then
{}^{p}d_{x}=d_{x}
for all
x\in W^{I}
.

Proof.

Lemma
5.5
implies the remaining non-simply-laced cases are equivalent to their Langlands dual Hecke categories (i.e. antispherical minuscule Hecke categories) when
2\in\Bbbk^{\times}
.
Since each simply-laced cominuscule flag variety is also minuscule, the claim follows from Theorem
5.2
.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

Unlike Theorem
5.2
, parabolic Koszul duality in characteristic 2 would not imply
{}^{2}d_{x}=d_{x}
for the cominuscule case.
As we will see, the 2-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis is considerably more complicated.
A geometric explanation for the characteristic 2 phenomena is conjectured in Section
10.3
.

6
Diagrammatic Hecke categories

We now recall the various diagrammatic categories we will use to determine the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases for the non-simply-laced, antispherical (co)minuscule Hecke categories.

6.1
Realisations

Recall the notion of a realisation
(\{\alphac_{s}\,|\,s\in S\}\subset\h,\{\alpha_{s}\,|\,s\in S\}\subset\h^{*})
of
W
as introduced in
[
EW
]
and subsequently refined in
[
Haz
]
.

Define
R
to be the graded ring
\Sym(\h^{*})
where
\h^{*}
is in degree
2
. The Coxeter group
W
acts on
R
via the contragradient representation. The Demazure operators
\partial_{s}:R\rightarrow R
are given by

\displaystyle\partial_{s}(f):=\frac{f-s(f)}{\alpha_{s}}~~~\text{for all }f\in R.

Further, we consider
\Bbbk
as an
R
-module via the isomorphism
\Bbbk\cong R/R_{+}
where
R_{+}
now denotes the ideal generated by polynomials of strictly positive degree.

The root datum of
G
gives rise to a realisation over
\Z
, known as the Cartan realisation.
We always take the realisation
\h
to be that obtained from the Cartan realisation by extending scalars to
\Bbbk
.
In particular, it can be of adjoint, simply-connected or universal type, in the sense of
[
Ric2
, §2.2.2]
.
Cartan realisations of universal or simply-connected type satisfy the ‘parabolic property’ of
[
LW
, §2.3]
, and Cartan realisations of universal or adjoint type always satisfy Demazure surjectivity.

6.2
The Elias-Williamson category

We now give an extremely terse account of the Elias-Williamson diagrammatic categories.
The reader not already familiar with these categories should consult
[
EMTW
]
.

Let
\sD_{\BS}(\h)
denote the diagrammatic
Bott-Samelson category
associated to the realisation
\h
, as introduced in
[
EW
]
.
It is a
\Bbbk
-linear, strict, monoidal category enriched over
R
-modules.
Objects
B_{\ux}
in
\sD_{\BS}(\h)
are indexed by (not necessarily reduced) expressions
\ux
.
The monoidal product is written as juxtaposition and defined by
B_{\ux}B_{\uy}=B_{\ux\uy}
where
\ux\uy
is the concatenation of
\ux
and
\uy
.
Morphisms in this category are given by planar diagrams, known as Soergel diagrams.
These diagrams are generated by vertices of the form

\begin{array}[]{CCC|C}\begin{array}[]{c}\textit{univalent}\\
\textit{vertex}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}\textit{trivalent}\\
\textit{vertex}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}2m_{st}\textit{-valent}\\
\textit{vertex}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}\textit{homogeneous}\\
\textit{polynomial}\end{array}\\

Each relation is homogeneous with respect to the degree of the Soergel graph, so
\Hom_{\sD_{\BS}(\h)}(B_{\ux},B_{\uy})
is a
\Z
-graded
\Bbbk
-module.
Following
[
ARV
]
, there is a category associated to
\sD_{\BS}(\h)
which has: objects
B_{\ux}(n)
, where
\ux
is an expression and
n\in\Z
; morphisms from
B_{\ux}(n)
to
B_{\uy}(m)
are degree
m-n
morphisms in
\Hom_{\sD_{\BS}(\h)}(B_{\ux},B_{\uy})
; and, a shift functor
(1)
.
We will interchangeably identify these categories.

The
(bi-equivariant) Elias-Williamson diagrammatic category

\DBE(\h)
is the graded, additive, Karoubian completion of
\sD_{\BS}(\h)
.
If we wish to emphasise the underlying ring
\Bbbk
, we write
\sD_{\text{BE}}(\h,\Bbbk)
.
It inherits a monoidal structure from
\sD_{\BS}(\h)
, which is again denoted by juxtaposition.
When
\Bbbk
is a complete local ring
\DBE(\h)
is Krull-Schmidt, and the indecomposable objects are parameterised by
B_{x}(n)
where
(x,n)\in W\times\Z
.
Moreover, each
B_{x}
is uniquely characterised by the properties:

(a)

if
\ux
is a reduced expression of
x
,
B_{x}
is a multiplicity-one direct summand of
B_{\ux}
;

(b)

if
\ell(\uy)<\ell(\ux)
,
B_{x}
is not a summand of
B_{\uy}
;

We define the graded Hom-space
\Hom^{\bullet}(B,B^{\prime})
as
\bigoplus_{n\in\Z}\Hom(B,B^{\prime}(n))
.
It is a free, finitely generated
R
-module.
Moreover, since we have taken
\h
to be the Cartan realisation of
G
, we have an equivalence of monoidal categories

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}:\Parity(B\backslash G/B)~~\longrightarrow~~\DBE(\h)

satisfying
\mathcal{D}(\cE_{x})\cong B_{x}
for all
x\in W
, see
[
RW1
, §10.3]
.

6.3
The diagrammatic antispherical category

We now recall the diagrammatic categorification of the antispherical module introduced in
[
RW1
, §4]
and generalised in
[
LW
]
.
We proceed in two steps.

First, consider the
(right-equivariant) Elias-Williamson diagrammatic category

\DRE(\h)
, which is defined as the category with the same objects as
\DBE(\h)
, and (graded) Hom-spaces are

\displaystyle\Hom^{\bullet}_{\DRE(\h)}(B,B^{\prime}):=\Bbbk\otimes_{R}\Hom^{\bullet}_{\DBE(\h)}(B,B^{\prime}).

This category is additive,
\Bbbk
-linear and has shift functor
(1)
induced by the shift functor on
\DBE(\h)
.
It is not monoidal, but is a module category over
\DBE(\h)
with the obvious monoidal action.
If
\Bbbk
is a complete local ring
\DRE(\h)
is Krull-Schmit, with indecomposable objects
B_{x}(n)
parameterised by
(x,n)\in W\times\Z
.
The forgetful functor
\For:\DBE(\h)\rightarrow\DRE(\h)
is essentially surjective and satisfies
\For(B_{x})\cong B_{x}
, hence our abuse of notation.
Since
\h
is the Cartan realisation of
G
, we have an equivalence

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}:\Parity(U\backslash G/B)~~\longrightarrow~~\DRE(\h)

satisfying
\mathcal{D}(\cE_{x})\cong B_{x}
for each
x\in W
; this follows from combining the arguments in
[
AR1
, §4.3]
,
[
Ric1
, §1.7]
and
[
EW
, §6.7]
.

The construction of the diagrammatic antispherical Hecke category is completely analogous to the quotient construction in Section
4.3
.
We define the
diagrammatic antispherical category

\DRA(\h)
as the category

\displaystyle\DRA(\h,I):=\DRE(\h)~/~\langle B_{x}~|~x\notin{}^{I}W\rangle_{\oplus,(1)}

where
\langle B_{x}~|~x\notin{}^{I}W\rangle_{\oplus,(1)}
denotes the full subcategory generated by the
B_{x}
under direct sums and shifts.
The category
\DRA(\h,I)
is graded, additive and a module category over
\DBE(\h)
.
When
\Bbbk
is a complete local ring,
\DRA(\h,I)
is Krull-Schmit with indecomposable objects
B_{x}(n)
parameterised by
(x,n)\in{}^{I}W\times\Z
.
Moreover, the quotient functor
q:\DRE(\h)\rightarrow\DRA(\h,I)
is essentially surjective and satisfies
q(B_{x})\cong B_{x}
, hence the abuse of notation.
Since
\h
is the Cartan realisation of
G
, the functors
\mathcal{D}
and
Av^{-1}
induce an equivalence

\displaystyle\Parity_{Wh,I}(G/B)~~\longrightarrow~~\DRA(\h,I)

satisfying
\mathcal{D}\circ Av^{-1}(\cE_{x})\cong B_{x}
for all
x\in{}^{I}W
.

We conclude by recalling that antispherical light-leaves were introduced in
[
LW
, §5]
.
These are morphisms in
\DRA(\h,I)
which are inductively constructed from the data of an
I
-parabolic Bruhat stroll on a subexpression
\ux^{e}\subseteq\ux
.
We assume the reader is familiar with this construction.

7
Diagrammatic antispherical (co)minuscule Hecke categories

We now determine the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases of antispherical (co)minuscule Hecke categories using diagrammatic methods.
These results hold in all characteristics, and facilitate greater insight into the structure of indecomposable objects in
\Parity_{Wh,I}(G/B)
.

The vast majority of this Section is devoted the antispherical Hecke category of the Lagrangian Grassmannian.
In Section
7.8
we consider odd dimensional quadric hypersurfaces.
The Langlands dual minuscule cases are treated simultaneously.

When we consider the diagrammatic antispherical Hecke category of the Lagrangian Grassmannian (resp. odd dimensional quadric hypersurfaces),
\h
will be a Cartan realisation of type
\tB_{n}
(resp.
\tC_{n}
) because Koszul duality requires we consider the Langlands dual realisation.

7.1
Minimal coset representatives

In Sections
7.1
-
7.7
, the Coxeter system
(W,S)
will always be type
\tB_{n}
and
I\subset S
will be type
\tA_{n-1}
.

We begin by establishing notation for
{}^{I}W
.
Standard references are
[
BM
]
or
[
BB
]
.
Our simple reflections are labelled as follows:

0
1
2
n-1
…

This ensures compatibility with the obvious inclusion
W_{\tB_{n}}\hookrightarrow W_{\tB_{n+1}}
.
Recall
W
can be realised as the signed permutations of
[\pm n]:=\{\pm 1,\dots,\pm n\}
, and
W_{I}
as the sign-symmetric permutations.
In terms of transpositions, this is

\displaystyle s_{0}=(-1,1)

and

\displaystyle s_{i}=(i,i+1)(-i-1,-i)\text{ when }0<i<n.

Each minimal coset representative
{}^{I}W
is fully commutative
[
Ste
, §6]
.
Many combinatorial objects index
{}^{I}W
, we briefly recall how they can be identified.

Subsets of
\{1,\dots,n\}
:
Let
[n]:=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and
\cP([n])
be the power-set of
[n]
.
The group
W
acts on
\cP([n])
via the action

\displaystyle xs_{i}=\begin{cases}(x\backslash\{i\})\cup\{i+1\}&\text{if }i\in x\text{ and }i+1\notin x,\\
x&\text{if }i,i+1\in x\text{ or }i,i+1\notin x,\text{ and}\\
(x\backslash\{i+1\})\cup\{i\}&\text{if }i+1\in x\text{ and }i\notin x.\end{cases}

(1)

where we identify
\{0\}
with
\emptyset
.
Clearly
W_{I}
stabilises
\emptyset
, and it is easily checked if
j\in x\subseteq[n]
is minimal and
i<j
, then
x(s_{0}s_{1}\dots s_{i-1})=x\cup\{i\}
.
Hence
W
acts transitively, and we can identify
{}^{I}W
with
\cP([n])
.
Under this identification
\id\in{}^{I}W
corresponds to
\emptyset
and
w_{I}w_{0}\in{}^{I}W
corresponds to
[n]
.

Strictly decreasing sequences
:
Given
\{t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{k}\}\subseteq[n]
we impose an order on the elements by requiring
t_{1}>t_{2}>\dots>t_{k}
. Then the sequence
(t_{1},t_{s},\dots,t_{k})
is strictly decreasing.

Partitions
:
Given a strictly decreasing sequence
(t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{k})
, we can associate the (left justified) partition with rows of lengths
t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{k}
from top-to-bottom.

Partitions are endowed with a partial order called the
containment order
:
a partition
x=(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
contains
y=(t_{1}^{\prime},\dots,t_{l}^{\prime})
if
k\geq l
and
t_{i}\geq t_{i}^{\prime}
for each
1\leq i\leq l
.
A partition
\lambda=(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
is called
strict
if
t_{1}>\dots>t_{k}
.
Figure
3
depicts the containment order on strict partitions with longest part
5
.
The following fact is shown in
[
BM
, Lemma 44]
.

Lemma 7.1
.

The containment order on strict partitions with longest part
n
is isomorphic, as a poset, to the Bruhat order on
{}^{I}W
.

The Bruhat poset in types

For
x\in{}^{I}W
we consider the associated partition.
Filling each box with the number of boxes to the left in that row
6
6

6

This is sometimes referred to as the right-arm length of the box.

defines a tableau.
Reading the entries of the tableau left-to-right, top-to-bottom gives a reduced expression for
x\in{}^{I}W
, which we call the
row reduced expression
.
This is a special case of
[
BM
, Theorem 8]
.

Given
x\in{}^{I}W
and the tableau constructed above, we construct a
shifted tableau
by shifting the
i^{\text{th}}
row
2(i-1)
boxes to the right.
The
column reduced expression
is obtained by reading the entries of the tableau top-to-bottom, left-to-right.

One sees the column reduced expression is reduced, as it can be obtained from the row reduced expression by repeatedly applying the commutative braid relation
st=ts
to each
s_{i}
until
s_{i}
is either:
on the far left (in which case
i=0
);
or adjacent to either
s_{0}
,
s_{1}
or
s_{i+2}
.

respectively. The row reduced expression is
\ux=s_{0}s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}s_{4}s_{5}~s_{0}s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}~s_{0}s_{1}~s_{0}
, while the column reduced expression is
\ux=s_{0}~s_{1}~s_{2}s_{0}~s_{3}s_{1}~s_{4}s_{2}s_{0}~s_{5}s_{3}s_{1}~s_{0}
.

Our rationale for considering these reduced expressions is as follows:
the column reduced expression is well-suited for studying subexpressions, whereas the row reduced expression is better-suited for inductive arguments on the length of elements.

We freely and implicitly identify elements of
{}^{I}W
with subsets of
[n]
, and the corresponding partitions and tableaux.
Given
x=\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{k}\}
we can consider its:

•

Length
:
\ell(x)=\sum_{i}t_{i}
, which is the usual length function on
{}^{I}W
, and

•

Cardinality
:
|x|=k
, which is the cardinality of the subset corresponding to
x
.

Finally, note the row (resp. column) reduced expression
\ux
arises as the concatenation of the expressions for each row (resp. column) in the tableau (resp. shifted tableau).

7.2
Defect-0 subexpressions

We now identify a set of defect-0 subexpressions of
\ux
for the row and column reduced expression of
x
.
In Section
7.5
it will be shown these are
all
defect-0 subexpressions of
\ux
.

Fix
x\subseteq[n]
and define
E(x)\subset x
as

\displaystyle E(x):=\{t\in x~|~t\text{ is even and }|\{t^{\prime}\in x~|~t>t^{\prime}\geq k\}|>|\{t^{\prime}\notin x~|~t>t^{\prime}\geq k\}|\text{ for all }t>k\geq 1\}.

Ordering the elements of
x=\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{k}\}
shows

\displaystyle E(x)=\{t_{i}\in x~|~t_{i}\text{ is even and }t_{i}-t_{i+j}\leq 2j-1\text{ for all }1\leq j\leq t_{i}/2\}.

(2)

If
t_{i}\in E(x)
, the non-shifted teableau for
x
must have the rightmost box of row
i
marked
t_{i}-1
, row
i+j
must contain boxes marked
t_{i}-2j-1
and
t_{i}-2j
when
0<j<t_{i}/2
, and row
t_{i+t_{i}/2}
must contain a box marked
0
.
We call the skew-partition formed by such boxes a
length-
t_{i}
1-2-1-tableau

\underline{121}_{t_{i}}
.
Note
\underline{121}_{t_{i}}
only depends on
t_{i}
, not
i
;
i
determines how
\underline{121}_{t_{i}}
is contained inside
x
.
For example,
\underline{121}_{6}
is

5

3

4

1

2

0

Since the partition
x
is strict, there is no box to the right of
t_{i}-1
in row
i
, or
t_{i}-2
in row
i+1
.

Recall the shifted tableau for
x
is obtained from the non-shifted tableau by shifting row
i
by
2(i-1)
boxes to the right.
After shifting, the skew-tableau
\underline{121}_{t}
becomes a two-column, skew tableau
\uu_{t}
, where the left column consists of boxes marked with
t-1
,
t-3
,
\dots

1
read top-to-bottom, while the right column consists of boxes marked with
t-2
,
t-4
,
\dots
,
0
read top-to-bottom, and the right column is shifted-down by a box.
For example
\uu_{6}
is

5

3

4

1

2

0

Reading the entries of
u_{t}
top-to-bottom, left-to-right defines an expression

\displaystyle\uu_{t}=s_{t-1}s_{t-3}\dots s_{3}s_{1}~~s_{t-2}s_{t-4}\dots s_{2}s_{0}

which is reduced as each simple reflection appears at most once.
We write
u_{t}
for
\uu_{t\bullet}
.

Examples are provided for the sanity of the reader.

.

x=\{6,5,4,3,2,1\}
, then
E(x)=\{6,4,2\}
.
The tableaux corresponding to
x
are

where we have marked
u_{6}
in
green
,
u_{4}
in
red
, and
u_{2}
in
blue
.
Their reduced expressions are
\uu_{6}=s_{5}s_{3}s_{1}~s_{4}s_{2}s_{0}
,
\uu_{4}=s_{3}s_{1}~s_{2}s_{0}
, and
\uu_{2}=s_{1}~s_{0}
respectively.

The next example demonstrates boxes can appear above the left column of
\uu_{i}
.

Example 7.4
.

Let
x=\{6,4,2,1\}
, then
E(x)=\{2\}
.
The tableaux corresponding to

are

with
u_{2}
marked in
red
.
The reduced expression is
\uu_{2}=s_{1}~s_{0}
.

The final example demonstrates that
x
can have boxes to the right of some
u_{i}
which are not part of any
u_{j}
.

Example 7.5
.

x=\{10,9,8,7,3,1\}
, then

. The tableaux corresponding to

with
u_{10}
marked in
red
.
The reduced expression is
\uu_{10}=s_{9}s_{7}s_{5}s_{3}s_{1}~s_{8}s_{6}s_{4}s_{2}s_{0}
.

Now fix
x=\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{l}\}
and
E=\{t_{i_{1}}>\dots>t_{i_{k}}\}\subseteq E(x)
.
The subset
E
determines a sequence of disjoint skew-partitions
u_{t_{i}}
in the shifted partition of
x
, each with a reduced expression
\uu_{t_{i}}
.
This allows us to write the column reduced expression
\ux
of
x
as the concatenation of a sequence of expressions

\displaystyle\ux=\ur_{1}\uu_{t_{i_{1}}}\ur_{2}\uu_{t_{i_{2}}}\dots\ur_{k}\uu_{t_{i_{k}}}\ur_{k+1}

where
\ur_{1}
is the expression formed by taking all terms of
\ux
prior to the commencement of
\uu_{t_{i_{1}}}
,
\ur_{2}
is the expression formed by taking all terms of
\ux
proceeding
\uu_{t_{i_{1}}}
and preceding
\uu_{t_{i_{2}}}
, and so on.
Note that each
r_{i}
depends on both
x
and
E
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Fix
x\in{}^{I}W
and
E=\{t_{i}\}\subseteq E(x)
.
Then
\ur_{1}\ur_{2}
is a reduced expression for
x\backslash\{t_{i}\}
.

The following example is more enlightening than the proof.

Example 7.7
.

Let
x=\{10,9,8,7,3,1\}
, as in Example
7.5
, and
E=\{10\}
.
The expression
\ur_{1}\ur_{2}
corresponds to deleting the skew-partition
u_{10}
from
x
, then shifting the tableau
r_{2}
up until it joins
r_{1}
.

Before we prove the Lemma, we first introduce some notation.
For non-negative integers
a\leq b
define

\displaystyle\uw_{a}=s_{0}s_{1}\dots s_{a-1},

\displaystyle\uw_{a,b}=s_{a}s_{a+1}\dots s_{b-2}s_{b-1}.

Write
w_{a}
,
w_{a,b}
for the corresponding elements of
W
.
Note
\uw_{a}
is the expression obtained by reading the terms in a length-
a
row of any tableau.

Proof.

Fix
x=\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{k}\}
.
Recall
t_{i}
is even; set
j=t_{i}/2
.
By definition of the 1-2-1 tableau we have

\displaystyle\ur_{1}

\displaystyle=\uw_{t_{1}}\dots\uw_{t_{i-1}}\uw_{t_{i}-1}\uw_{t_{i}-3}\dots\uw_{3}\uw_{1},

\displaystyle\ur_{2}

\displaystyle=\uw_{t_{i}-1,t_{i+1}}\uw_{t_{i}-3,t_{i+2}}\dots\uw_{1,t_{i+j}}\uw_{t_{i+j+1}}\uw_{t_{i+j+2}}\dots\uw_{t_{k}}.

Note
\uw_{a}\uw_{a,b}=\uw_{b}
and
w_{a,b}
commutes with
w_{c}
for any
c<a
.
Consequently

\displaystyle(\ur_{1}\ur_{2})_{\bullet}

\displaystyle=w_{t_{1}}\dots w_{t_{i-1}}(w_{t_{i}-1}w_{t_{i}-1,t_{i+1}})\dots(w_{1}w_{1,t_{i+j}})w_{t_{i+j+1}}\dots w_{t_{k}}

\displaystyle=w_{t_{1}}\dots w_{t_{i-1}}w_{t_{i+1}}\dots w_{t_{i+j}}w_{t_{i+j+1}}\dots w_{t_{k}}

\displaystyle=w_{t_{1}}\dots w_{t_{i-1}}w_{t_{i+1}}\dots w_{t_{k}}

which is the element
x\backslash\{t_{i}\}
.
Note that
\ell(x\backslash\{t_{i}\})=\ell(x)-t_{i}
, which is the length of the expression
\ur_{1}\ur_{2}
.
Hence
\ur_{1}\ur_{2}
is reduced.
∎

Given an expression
\uz
, denote by
\uz^{0}
(resp.
\uz^{1}
) the subexpression where each index is 0 (resp.
1
).
For each
E\subseteq E(x)
we define an associated subexpression
\ux^{E}\subseteq\ux
as

\displaystyle\ux^{E}=\ur_{1}^{1}\uu_{t_{i_{1}}}^{0}\ur_{2}^{1}\uu_{t_{i_{2}}}^{0}\dots\ur_{k}^{1}\uu_{t_{i_{k}}}^{0}\ur_{k+1}^{1}.

Proposition 7.8
.

Fix
x\in{}^{I}W
and
E\subseteq E(x)
.
The subexpression
\ux^{E}\subseteq\ux
is
I
-parabolic, with defect
0
and no
\begin{smallmatrix}\da\\
1\end{smallmatrix}
’s occurring in the Bruhat stroll.
Moreover,
\left(\ux^{E}\right)_{\bullet}=x\backslash E
.

Proof.

Fix
x=\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{l}\}
.
We prove the claim by induction on
|E|
.
If
|E|=0
, the claim is trivial.

Suppose
E=\{t_{i}\}
.
Then
\ux=\ur_{1}\uu_{t_{i}}\ur_{2}
and
\ux^{E}=\ur_{1}^{1}\uu_{t_{i}}^{0}\ur_{2}^{1}
.
Since
\ux
is reduced,
\ur_{1}
must also be reduced.
In particular every decoration of
\ur_{1}^{1}
has the form
\begin{smallmatrix}\ua\\
1\end{smallmatrix}
.
It is clear
\ur_{1}
is a reduced expression for

\displaystyle r_{1}=\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{i-1}>t_{i}-1>t_{i}-3>\dots>3>1\}.

The action given in Equation
1
implies if
j\leq t_{i}-1
then

\displaystyle r_{1}s_{j}>r_{1}

\displaystyle~~~\text{ if }j\text{ is odd, and}

\displaystyle r_{1}s_{j}<r_{1}

\displaystyle~~~\text{ if }j\text{ is even.}

Hence every term in
\uu_{t_{i}}^{0}
with odd subscript has decoration
\begin{smallmatrix}\ua\\
0\end{smallmatrix}
, and every term with even subscript has decoration
\begin{smallmatrix}\da\\
0\end{smallmatrix}
.
Consequently the subexpression
\ur_{1}^{1}\uu_{t_{i}}^{0}
has defect 0.
Lemma
7.6
implies
(\ur_{1}^{1}\uu_{t_{i}}^{0}\ur_{2}^{1})_{\bullet}=x\backslash\{t_{i}\}
.
Since
\ur_{1}\ur_{2}
is reduced and evaluates to
x\backslash\{t_{i}\}
, it follows
\ux^{E}
has no
\begin{smallmatrix}\da\\
1\end{smallmatrix}
’s.

Now suppose
E=\{t_{i_{1}}>\dots>t_{i_{k}}\}
for
k>1
.
We can write
\ux
as the concatenation

\displaystyle\ux=\ur_{1}\uu_{t_{i_{1}}}\dots\uu_{t_{i_{k-1}}}\ur_{k}\uu_{t_{i_{k}}}\ur_{k+1}

Define
\uy=\ur_{1}\uu_{t_{1}}\dots\ur_{k}
and
y=\uy_{\bullet}
.
As
\ux
is a reduced expression for
x
it follows
\uy
is a reduced expression for
y
and
y\in{}^{I}W
.
Moreover,
E\backslash\{t_{i_{k}}\}\subset E(y)
.
By induction
\uy^{E\backslash\{t_{i_{k}}\}}
is a defect-0 subexpression of
\uy
that has no
\begin{smallmatrix}\da\\
1\end{smallmatrix}
’s in its Bruhat stroll and evaluates to
y\backslash(E\backslash\{t_{i_{k}}\})
.
Now observe that

\displaystyle\ux^{E}=\uy^{E\backslash\{t_{i_{k}}\}}\uu_{t_{i_{k}}}^{0}\ur_{k+1}^{1}

so we can argue exactly as in the base case to prove the claim.
∎

In Section
7.5
we prove every defect-0 subexpression of
\ux
has the form
\ux^{E}
where
E\subseteq E(x)
.

7.3
Entries in the local intersection form

We now construct light-leaves associated to the subexpressions
\ux^{E}
in the diagrammatic category
\DRA(\h,I)
.
Then we determine entries in the local intersection form associated to those light-leaves.
Recall
\h
is a Cartan realisation of type
\tB_{n}
.
Our labelling conventions imply
\left\langle\alphac_{0},\alpha_{1}\right\rangle=-2
.

We call an even-indexed (resp. odd-indexed) simple reflection an
even reflection (resp. odd reflection)
.
Given the important role that the parity of a simple reflection plays, we colour our simple reflections as follows:

0
1
2
3
4
5
6
…

Fix
x
and
E\subseteq E(x)
.
By construction
\ur_{1}
is formed from alternating decreasing sequences of even and odd reflections.
In general
\ur_{1}
can end with either a sequence of even or odd reflections.
If
\ur_{1}
ends with a sequence of odd reflections, they commute with every subsequent reflection in the column reduced expression
\ux
of
x
.
Consequently we only consider the case where every
\ur_{1}
ends in a sequence of even reflections.
The light-leaf corresponding to
\ur_{1}^{1}
is the identity morphism on
B_{\ur_{1}}
as
\ur_{1}
is reduced.
A prototypical light-leaf corresponding to
\ur_{1}^{1}
has the form

That is, it is the identity morphism on
\ur_{1}
, followed by a sequence of odd-coloured up-dots, followed by a sequence (of the same length) of even-coloured trivalent vertices.

Now consider the morphism
LL_{\ur_{1}\uu_{1},E}\circ\Gamma\Gamma^{\ur_{1}\uu_{1},E}
, which is the composition of the light leaf
LL_{\ur_{1}\uu_{1},E}
with the morphism obtained by reflecting
LL_{\ur_{1}\uu_{1},E}
along a horizontal axis.
Note the following rather trivial identity holds for
a<c
by the twisted Leibniz rule

\displaystyle\partial_{s_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textit{2a}}}}\left(\prod^{c}_{b\geq a}\alpha_{s_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\textit{2b+1}}}}\right)=\left\langle\alphac_{s_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\textit{2a}}}},\alpha_{s_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\textit{2a+1}}}}\right\rangle\prod_{b>a}^{c}\alpha_{s_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\textit{2b+1}}}}.

(3)

So, it is easy to describe the action of certain Demazure operators on products of odd-indexed simple roots.
Consequently we have

\displaystyle LL_{\ur_{1}\uu_{1},E}\circ\Gamma\Gamma^{\ur_{1}\uu_{1},E}=\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox to29.25pt{\vbox to86.16pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-16.67162pt\lower-43.07913pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

where the second equality follows from the fact that all even reflections commute; the third equality from repeatedly applying the polynomial forcing relation and Equation
3
; and, the fourth uses that
\left\langle\alphac_{0},\alpha_{1}\right\rangle=-2
in type
\tB_{n}
.

Suppose
E=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}
where
k>1
.
The subexpression corresponding to
E
is

\displaystyle\ux^{E}=\ur_{1}^{1}\uu_{t_{1}}^{0}\ur_{2}^{1}\dots\ur_{k}^{1}\uu_{t_{k}}^{0}\ur_{k+1}^{1}.

Proposition
7.8
implies each
\ur_{j}
is exclusively decorated with
\begin{smallmatrix}\ua\\
1\end{smallmatrix}
’s, so the corresponding light leaf is the identity on each
B_{\ur_{j}}
.
Again, we can reduce to the case where each
\ur_{j}
ends in a sequence of even reflections, as any odd sequence of odd reflections ending
\ur_{j}
will commute with every simple reflection to the right of
\ur_{j}
in
\ux
.
For example, if
\ur_{2}\neq\emptyset
, a typical light leaf corresponding to
\ur_{1}^{1}\uu_{t_{i_{1}}}^{0}\ur_{2}^{1}
has the form

On the other hand, each
\uu_{t_{i}}^{0}
is a sequence of odd reflections each decorated with
\begin{smallmatrix}\ua\\
0\end{smallmatrix}
’s, followed by a sequence of even reflections each decorated with
\begin{smallmatrix}\da\\
0\end{smallmatrix}
’s.
Since both
\ur_{j}
and
\uu_{t_{j}}
end in a sequence of even reflections (with subscripts at least as large as every even reflection appearing to the right in
\ux
), the even reflections in
\uu_{t_{i}}
must form trivalent vertices with the even reflections in
\ur_{i}
if
\ur_{i}\neq\emptyset
, or
\uu_{t_{i-1}}
if
\ur_{i}=\emptyset
.
Thus, typical light leaves for
\ur_{1}\uu_{t_{i}}\ur_{2}\uu_{t_{2}}
have the form

\ur_{2}\neq\emptyset
, and in the second
\ur_{2}=\emptyset
.
Repeatedly applying Equation
7.3
to the composition
LL_{\ux,E}\circ\Gamma\Gamma^{\ux,E}
shows:

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
x\in{}^{I}W
and
\ux
be the column reduced expression.
Fix
E=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}\subseteq E(x)
, and recall the decomposition
\ux=\ur_{1}\uu_{t_{1}}\dots\ur_{k}\uu_{t_{k}}\ur_{k+1}
induced by
E
, then

\displaystyle LL_{\ux,E}\circ\Gamma\Gamma^{\ux,E}=2^{|E|}(-1)^{\ell(E)/2}\id_{B_{\ur_{1}\ur_{2}\ur_{3}\dots\ur_{k+1}}}

where
\id_{B_{\ur_{1}\ur_{2}\ur_{3}\dots\ur_{k+1}}}
is the identity morphism on the Bott-Samelson object
B_{\ur_{1}\ur_{2}\ur_{3}\dots\ur_{k+1}}
,
|E|=k
and
\ell(E)=\sum_{i}t_{i}
.

We illustrate
7
7

7

Pun intended.
the Proposition with an aesthetically pleasing example.

Example 7.10
.

If
x=\{6,5,4,3,2,1\}
then
E(x)=\{6,4,2\}
.
Set
E=E(x)
.

LL_{\ux,E}\circ\Gamma\Gamma^{\ux,E}

For the sake of completeness we emphasise
|E|=3
and
\ell(E)/2=(6+4+2)/2=6
.

Remark 7.11
.

In type
\tC_{n}
we have
\left\langle\alphac_{0},\alpha_{1}\right\rangle=-1
.
Modifying the preceding argument shows

\displaystyle LL_{\ux,E}\circ\Gamma\Gamma^{\ux,E}=(-1)^{\ell(E)/2}\id_{B_{\ur_{1}\ur_{2}\ur_{3}\dots\ur_{k+1}}}.

Remark 7.12
.

Proposition
7.8
proves the Bruhat strolls associated to
\ux^{E}
contain no
\begin{smallmatrix}\da\\
1\end{smallmatrix}
’s.
Consequently a non-diagrammatic proof of Proposition
7.9
can be obtained using the nil-Hecke ring and the ‘gobbling morphisms’ of
[
HeW
]
.

7.4
The classical Kazhdan-Lusztig basis

We recall the description of the classical Kazhdan-Lusztig basis of
{}^{I}N
as determined in
[
LS
]
.
They use different combinatorial objects to index their minimal coset representatives, and consider the antispherical module of type
\tA_{n}\backslash\tD_{n+1}
.
Lemmata
5.1
and
5.5
imply the Kazhdan-Lusztig basis of type
\tA_{n}\backslash\tD_{n+1}
is the Kazhdan-Lusztig basis in type
\tA_{n-1}\backslash\tB_{n}
.
We also translate their results into our language of sets and partitions.

For this section only, we consider
x\in{}^{I}W
as an even cardinality subset of
[\tilde{n}]:=\{0,1,\dots,n\}
, instead of the usual
[n]:=\{1,\dots,n\}
.
We do this in the obvious way:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccl}\cP([n])&\hookrightarrow&\cP([\tilde{n}])\\
x&\mapsto&\begin{cases}x&\text{if }|x|\text{ is even, }\\
x\cup\{0\}&\text{if }|x|\text{ is odd. }\end{cases}\end{array}

For each
x\in{}^{I}W
, we construct a relation
\sim_{x}
on
[\tilde{n}]
in two steps.
If
i,j\in[\tilde{n}]
and
i\sim_{x}j
we say
i
and
j
are
x
-related
.

•

Step 1
: Say
i\sim_{x}j
if:
i>j
,
i\in x
,
j\notin x
, and
i
is the minimal number such that
|\{i^{\prime}\in x~|~i\geq i^{\prime}\geq j\}|=|\{j^{\prime}\notin x~|~i\geq j^{\prime}\geq j\}|
.

•

Step 2
: Enumerate those
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\in x
that were not joined in step one, such that
t_{1}<\dots<t_{k}
.
For any integer
j
, where
1\leq j\leq k/2
, say
t_{{2j-1}}\sim_{x}t_{2j}
.
Note that if
k
is odd, then
t_{k}
is not
x
-related to anything.

Example 7.13
.

Let
x=[4]\subset[\tilde{4}]
.
Note that
0\notin x
.
So
1\sim_{x}0
in Step 1 and
3\sim_{x}2
in Step
2
.
The element
4\in[\tilde{4}]
is not
x
-related to any element.

For each
x
-related pair
i,j\in[\tilde{n}]
where
i>j
, define maps

\displaystyle\phi_{i,j}^{x}(z)=\begin{cases}\left(z\backslash\{i\}\right)\cup\{j\}&\text{if }i,j\text{ are joined in step one, and}\\
z\backslash\{i,j\}&\text{if }i,j\text{ are joined in step two.}\end{cases}

If there is a sequence of maps
\phi^{x}_{i_{1},j_{1}},\dots,\phi^{x}_{i_{k},j_{k}}
satisfying
\phi^{x}_{i_{k},j_{k}}\circ\dots\circ\phi^{x}_{i_{1},j_{1}}(x)=y
, define
\#(y,x)
to be the minimal length of such a sequence. Otherwise, set
\#(x,y)=\infty
.

We can now state
[
LS
, §2.2]
by another name.

Proposition 7.14
.

Let
x,y\in{}^{I}W
, then

\displaystyle n_{y,x}=\begin{cases}v^{\#(y,x)}&\text{if }\#(y,x)<\infty,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We sketch how to translate our terminology into theirs.
For
x\subseteq[\tilde{n}]
, define a sequence of length
n+1
whose
(i+1)^{\text{th}}
term is
-
if
i\in x
and
+
if
i\notin x
.
This is the
\{-,+\}
-sequence of
[
LS
, §1]
corresponding to the image of
x
under the poset isomorphism between
({}^{\tA_{n}}W_{\tD_{n+1}},\leq)
and
({}^{\tA_{n-1}}W_{\tC_{n}},\leq)
.
To a
\{-,+\}
-sequence Lejczyk and Stroppel associate a ‘cup diagram’, see
[
LS
, §2.1]
.
A tedious definition chase shows that
i\sim_{x}j
in:

•

step 1 correspond to points in
\{1,\dots,n+1\}
which are connected by a cup in the cup diagram for the
\{-,+\}
-sequence associated to
x
, and

•

step 2 correspond to points in
\{\pm 1,\dots,\pm(n+1)\}
which are connected by a cup which crosses 0 in the cup diagram for the
\{-,+\}
-sequence associated to
x
.

Finally, the maps
\phi^{x}_{i,j}
correspond to ‘clockwise-oriented’ weights, as we insist
i>j
.

Remark 7.15
.

The maps
\phi^{x}_{i,j}
have an intuitive description in terms of partitions.
Fix
i>j
.
If
i\sim_{x}j
are linked in step 1 then
\phi^{x}_{i,j}
flips row
i
onto row
j
; recall that we have an empty row when
j=0
.
If
i\sim_{x}j
are linked in step 2 then
\phi^{x}_{i,j}
removes both rows
i
and
j
.
