So
d_{\{4,3,2,1\}}=\delta_{\{4,3,2,1\}}^{I}+v\delta_{\{4,3,2\}}^{I}+v\delta_{\{4,1\}}^{I}+v^{2}\delta_{\{4\}}^{I}
.

Observe
\#(y,x)=1
if and only if
y
is obtained from
x
by applying exactly one row flip or row removal.
Consequently, the symmetric difference of
x
and
y
is
\{i,j\}
where
i\sim_{x}j
.

7.5
Bott-Samelson basis

We now determine the Bott-Samelson basis of
{}^{I}N
.
The inductive construction utilises the
row reduced expression
of
x
.
However, since each
x\in{}^{I}W
is fully commutative, each result holds independent of the choice of reduced expression.

Lemma 7.17
.

If
x=\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{l}>i+1\}\in{}^{I}W
, then either
E(x)=E(xs_{i})
or
E(x)=E(xs_{i})\cup\{t_{j}\}
where
t_{j+t_{j}/2}=1
.

Proof.

By Equation
1
we have

\displaystyle xs_{i}=\begin{cases}\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{l}>i\}&\text{ if }i\geq 1,\\
\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{l}\}&\text{ if }i=0.\end{cases}

So
x
and
xs_{i}
only differ in their minimal elements.
In either case
x>xs_{i}
.
Note that for any
y\in{}^{I}W
, if
t_{j}\in E(y)
then
t_{j+t_{j}/2}\geq 1
, as row
(j+t_{j}/2)
in the partition corresponding to
y
is non-empty.
If
i\geq 1
then any
t\in E(x)
also satisfies the row-length condition in Equation
2
to be in
E(xs_{i})
and vice versa.
Hence
E(xs_{i})=E(x)
.

If
i=0
, then
x=\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{l}>1\}
and
xs_{0}=\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{l}\}
.
There are two possibilities to consider.

Suppose
t_{j}\in E(x)
is minimal and
t_{j+t_{j}/2}=1
, then
t_{j}\notin E(xs_{0})
as
|xs_{0}|=j+t_{j}/2-1
, i.e. the partition
xs_{0}
has too few parts.
Hence
E(x)=E(xs_{0})\cup\{t_{j}\}
.
Alternatively, suppose
t_{j}\in E(x)
is minimal and
t_{j+t_{j}/2}>1
, then
t_{j}\in E(xs_{0})
as the row-length condition in Equation
2
remains satisfied.
Hence
E(x)=E(xs_{0})
. ∎

We can now determine the Bott-Samelson bases for
{}^{I}N
.

Proposition 7.18
.

Fix
x\in{}^{I}W
, and let
\ux
be any reduced expression. Then

\displaystyle d_{\ux}=\sum_{E\subseteq E(x)}d_{x\backslash E}.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction on
\ell(\ux)
.
If
\ux=\emptyset
then
E(\id)=\emptyset
, and the claim is immediate.

Now fix
\ux
where
\ell(\ux)>0
and assume the claim holds for every reduced expression
\ux^{\prime}
where
\ell(\ux^{\prime})<\ell(\ux)
.
Let
x=\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{l}>i+1\}
, Equation
1
again implies

\displaystyle xs_{i}=\begin{cases}\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{l}>i\}&\text{ if }i\geq 1\\
\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{l}\}&\text{ if }i=0.\end{cases}

Clearly
x>xs_{i}
as the Bruhat order on
{}^{I}W
agrees with the containment order on partitions.
Minimality implies
i+1,i\notin E(x)
and
i+1,i\notin E(xs_{i})
.
For any
E\subset E(xs_{i})
set
y_{E}=xs_{i}\backslash E
.
Then we have
y_{E}s_{i}=x\backslash E
, as
i
is in both
y_{E}
and
x\backslash E
, and
i+1
is in neither
y_{E}
nor
x\backslash E
.
The induction hypothesis implies

\displaystyle d_{\ux}=d_{\underline{xs}_{i}}b_{s_{i}}=\sum_{E\subseteq E(xs_{i})}d_{y_{E}}b_{s_{i}}=\sum_{E\subseteq E(xs_{i})}d_{x\backslash E}+\sum_{E\subseteq E(xs_{i})}\sum_{zs_{i}<z}\mu(z,y_{E})d_{z}

where
\mu(z,y_{E})
is the coefficient of
v
in
n_{z,y_{E}}
.
By Proposition
7.14
we know
\mu(z,y_{E})=1
if
\#(z,y_{E})=1
and
\mu(z,y_{E})=0
otherwise.
Hence

\displaystyle d_{\ux}=\sum_{E\subseteq E(xs_{i})}d_{x\backslash E}+\sum_{E\subseteq E(xs_{i})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}zs_{i}<z\\
\#(z,y_{E})=1\end{subarray}}d_{z}.

(8)

The condition
zs_{i}<z
is equivalent to either:
i+1\in z
and
i\notin z
when
i>0
; or
1\in z
when
i=0
.

Observe that if
i>0
the elements
i+1
and
i
are not
y_{E}
-related as
i+1\notin y_{E}
and it is necessary that the greater of the two elements be in
y_{E}
.
It follows that
\#(z,y_{E})\geq 2
for any
z
satisfying
zs_{i}<z
.
Consequently

\displaystyle d_{\ux}=\sum_{E\subseteq E(xs_{i})}d_{x\backslash E}=\sum_{E\subseteq E(x)}d_{x\backslash E}

where the second equality follows from Lemma
7.17
.
This proves the claim when
i>0
.

If
i=0
, then
xs_{0}=\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{l}\}
where
t_{l}\geq 2
.
By Equation
1
,
zs_{0}<z
is equivalent to
1\in z
.
So, it follows that
\#(z,y_{E})=1
if and only if there exists
t\in y_{E}
such that
t\sim_{y_{E}}1
and
\phi_{t,1}^{y_{E}}(y_{E})=z
.
Suppose such a
t
exists.
Since
1\notin y_{E}
, it must be that
t\sim_{y_{E}}1
in Step 1.
By definition
t
must be the minimal element in
y_{E}
such that
|\{j\in y_{E}~|~t\geq j\geq 1\}|=|\{j\notin y_{E}~|~t\geq j\geq 1\}|
.
Since
t
is minimal,
t
is strictly smaller than the minimal element of
E(xs_{0})
as
t^{\prime}\in E(xs_{0})
requires
|\{j\in xs_{0}~|~t^{\prime}\geq j\geq 1\}|>|\{j\notin xs_{0}~|~t^{\prime}\geq j\geq 1\}|
.
Hence
t
is determined by
xs_{0}
.
Consequently if
t
exists for some
y_{E}=xs_{0}\backslash E
, it exists for every
y_{E}=xs_{0}\backslash E
; that is, it is independent of the subset
E
.
Further,
t\in E(x)
because
x=xs_{0}\cup\{1\}
.
Hence
E(x)=E(xs_{0})\cup\{t\}
by Lemma
7.17
.
Finally, note that
z=(y_{E}\backslash\{t\})\cup\{1\}=x\backslash(E\cup\{t\})
as
t\sim_{y_{E}}1
are related in Step 1.
Hence

\displaystyle d_{\ux}=\sum_{E\subseteq E(xs_{0})}d_{xs_{0}\backslash E}b_{s_{0}}=\sum_{E\subseteq E(xs_{0})}(d_{x\backslash E}+d_{x\backslash(E\cup\{t\})})=\sum_{E\subseteq E(x)}d_{x\backslash E}

proving the claim.

If no such
t
exists, then the claim is immediate from Equation
8
and Lemma
7.17
. ∎

7.6
The
2
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis

We now determine the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis using local intersection forms.
We quickly recall the construction of local intersection forms from
[
JW
,
GJW
,
EMTW
]
.

Let
\sD_{\text{RA}}^{\BS}(\h,I)
denote the full subcategory of Bott-Samelson objects in
\DRA(\h,I)
.
Define

\displaystyle\sD^{\not<y}_{\text{RA}}(\h,I):=\sD_{\text{RA}}^{\BS}(\h,I)~/~\langle B_{\uz}|z<y\rangle_{(1),\oplus}.

for each
y\in W
.
The category is enriched over
\Z
-graded
R
-modules.
We denote by
\Hom_{\not<y}^{\bullet}(B,B^{\prime})
the graded Hom spaces and
\Hom_{\not<y}^{i}(B,B^{\prime})
the degree-
i
morphisms.
Since any non-identity endomorphisms of
B_{\uy}
factor through some
B_{\uz}
where
z<y
, we have
\Endo^{\bullet}_{\not<y}(B_{\uy})\cong R
and
\Endo^{0}_{\not<y}(B_{\uy})\cong\Bbbk
.
Hence, for any Bott-Samelson object
B_{\ux}
, we define the
degree-
i
local intersection form

I_{y,\ux}^{i}
as:

\begin{array}[]{ccccccC}I_{y,\ux}^{i}:&\Hom_{\not<y}^{i}(B_{\ux},B_{\uy})\times\Hom^{-i}_{\not<y}(B_{\uy},B_{\ux})&\longrightarrow&\Endo_{\not<y}^{\bullet}(B_{\uy})&\longrightarrow&\Endo_{\not<y}^{0}(B_{\uy})\cong\Bbbk\\
\\
&(f,g)&\longmapsto&f\circ g&\longmapsto&(f\circ g)_{0}\end{array}

where
(f\circ g)_{0}
denotes the degree-0 component of
f\circ g
.
The importance of the local intersection form stems from the following Lemma.

Lemma 7.19
.

Let
\Bbbk
be a complete local ring, and
\bF
the residue field.
For objects
B_{y}
and
B_{\ux}
in
\DRA(\h,I,\Bbbk)
, the graded multiplicity of
B_{y}
in
B_{\ux}
is

\displaystyle\grrk I^{\bullet}_{y,\ux}=\sum_{i}\rank(I_{y,\ux}^{i}\otimes\bF)v^{i}.

Proof.

This is
[
GJW
, §5]
for
\DBE(\h)
, the idempotent lifting arguments carry over to
\DRA(\h,I)
.
∎

We can now determine the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis.

Theorem 7.20
.

Let
\h
be a Cartan realisation type
\tB_{n}
over a field or a complete local ring
\Bbbk
, and
I\subset S
of type
\tA_{n-1}
.
The
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis of
\sD_{\text{RA}}(\h,I,\Bbbk)
is

\displaystyle{}^{p}d_{x}=\begin{cases}d_{\ux}&\text{if }2\notin\Bbbk^{\times},\text{ and }\\
d_{x}&\text{if }2\in\Bbbk^{\times},\end{cases}

where
\ux
is any reduced expression of
x
.

Proof.

For any expression
\uz
, not necessarily reduced, Lemma
7.19
shows that after taking the diagrammatic character (see
[
LW
, §6]
) we have

\displaystyle d_{\uz}=\sum_{w\in W}\grrk I^{\bullet}_{y,\ux}~d_{w}.

Hence it suffices to compute the local intersection forms only at those
y\leq x
such that
d_{y}
has non-zero coefficient in
d_{\ux}
.
By Proposition
7.18
, we need only check the local intersection forms
I_{x\backslash E,\ux}^{\bullet}
for each
E\subseteq E(x)
.
Since the sum in Proposition
7.18
is multiplicity free and concentrated in degree 0, it follows that the
I_{x\backslash E,\ux}^{\bullet}
is concentrated in degree 0, i.e.
I_{x\backslash E,\ux}^{\bullet}=I^{0}_{x\backslash E,\ux}
, and determined by
LL_{\ux,E}\circ\Gamma\Gamma^{\ux,E}
.
This composition was determined in Proposition
7.9
, which implies the local intersection form is the
1\times 1
matrix

\displaystyle I_{x\backslash E,\ux}^{0}=\left[2^{|E|}(-1)^{\ell(E)/2}\right].

Hence, if
E\neq\emptyset
we have

\displaystyle\grrk\left(I_{x\backslash E,\ux}^{\bullet}\otimes\bF\right)=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }2\notin\Bbbk,\text{ and }\\
1&\text{ if }2\in\Bbbk.\end{cases}

Hence the claim.
∎

Remark 7.21
.

Suppose
\Bbbk
is a complete local ring such that
2\notin\Bbbk
and let
\bK
denote its fraction field.
Recall
w_{I}w_{0}=[n]
.
It is easily seen that
|E(w_{I}w_{0})|=\lfloor n/2\rfloor
and
\ell(w_{I}w_{0})=\binom{n+1}{2}
.
If we denote by
\bK(B_{w_{I}w_{0}})
the object obtained by extending scalars, then Theorem
7.20
implies

\displaystyle\bK(B_{w_{I}w_{0}})\cong\bigoplus_{y\in E(w_{I}w_{0})}{}^{\bK}B_{w_{I}w_{0}\backslash E}

which has
2^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
summands.
Varying
n
produces the first family in finite Hecke categories of indecomposable objects
B
whose number of summands under extension of scalars grows exponentially relative to their length.
8
8

8

Let
\Bbbk
be a complete local ring with residue field of characteristic
p
, and fraction field
\bK
.
If
\h
is a realisation of type
\tI_{2}(m)
over
\Bbbk
, then any
x\in W
has the property
\bK(B_{x})
has
2^{k}
indecomposable summands, where
k
is the number of non-zero digits in the
p
-adic expansion of
\ell(x)
.
In particular, the number of summands grows (at most) linearly in the length of
x
.

Remark 7.22
.

The argument in Theorem
7.20
shows that in type
\tC_{n}
, for any
x\in{}^{I}W
and
E\subseteq E(x)
, the local intersection form is

\displaystyle I_{x\backslash E,\ux}^{0}=\left[(-1)^{\ell(E)/2}\right].

Hence the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis for the odd orthogonal grassmannian is independent of
p
.

Remark 7.23
.

The Bott-Samelson basis of
{}^{I}N
in type
\tC_{n}
was studied in
[
BDF+
]
, where it was shown that when
d_{\ux}
is expressed in terms of the standard basis each coefficient is a monic monomial.
Obviously their result extends to type
\tB_{n}
.
Combined with Theorem
7.20
, this shows all
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomials for
\DRA(\h,I)
are monoic monomials.

7.7
Degree-0 endomorphism algebras

We conclude by noting that the subalgebra
\Endo^{0}(B_{x})\subset\Endo^{\bullet}(B_{x})
has a beautiful combinatorial description.
This allows us to explicitly determine the idempotents which project from
B_{\ux}
onto
B_{x}
.
In this section we fix
x\in{}^{I}W
and define
\varphi_{E}:=\Gamma\Gamma^{\ux,E}\circ LL_{\ux,E}
for each
E\subseteq E(x)
.
We continue to assume
\Bbbk
is a field or a complete local ring.

Lemma 7.24
.

If
2\notin\Bbbk^{\times}
, then the morphisms
\{\varphi_{E}~|~E\subseteq E(x)\}
are a basis of
\Endo^{0}(B_{x})
.

Proof.

This is immediate from Theorem
7.20
and the fact that double-light-leaves are a basis of
B_{\ux}
, see
[
LW
, §5]
.
∎

Example 7.25
.

x=\{6,5,4,3,2,1\}
. Then
E(x)=\{6,4,2\}
and
\Endo^{0}(B_{x})
has a basis given by the following morphisms:

Lemma 7.26
.

For any
\Bbbk
and any
x\in{}^{I}W
the algebra
\Endo^{0}(B_{x})
is commutative.

The proof is a simple application of Gel’fand’s trick.

Proof.

The algebra
\Endo^{0}(B_{x})
has an antiinvolution
\bD:\Endo^{0}(B_{x})\rightarrow\Endo^{0}(B_{x})
given by reflecting diagrams about their horizontal midpoint.
If
2\notin\Bbbk^{\times}
, denote the structure constants for the basis
\{\varphi_{E}\}
by
\{c_{E,E^{\prime}}^{E^{\prime\prime}}\}
.
Since each
\varphi_{E}
is symmetric about its horizontal midpoint we see

\displaystyle\varphi_{E^{\prime}}\varphi_{E}=\bD(\varphi_{E}\varphi_{E^{\prime}})=\bD\left(\sum_{E^{\prime\prime}}c^{E^{\prime\prime}}_{E,E^{\prime}}\varphi_{E^{\prime\prime}}\right)=\sum_{E^{\prime\prime}}c^{E^{\prime\prime}}_{E,E^{\prime}}\bD(\varphi_{E^{\prime\prime}})=\sum_{E^{\prime\prime}}c^{E^{\prime\prime}}_{E,E^{\prime}}\varphi_{E^{\prime\prime}}=\varphi_{E}\varphi_{E^{\prime}}.

Hence
\Endo^{0}(B_{x})
is commutative.
If
2\in\Bbbk^{\times}
, then
\Endo^{0}(B_{x})\cong\Bbbk
and the claim is trivial.
∎

Remark 7.27
.

We note the following generalisations of Lemma
7.26
:

(a)

Let
\Bbbk
be a complete local ring with fraction field
\bK
.
The preceding argument shows for any Hecke category with double-light-leaf basis, if
\bK(B)
has a multiplicity-free decomposition then
\Endo^{0}(B)
is commutative.

(b)

If we fix
x
and require every
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomial
{}^{p}h_{y,x}
,
{}^{p}m_{y,x}
, or
{}^{p}n_{y,x}
be a monic monomial (as we vary
y
), then the graded endomorphism algebra
\Endo^{\bullet}(B_{x})
is commutative.

We can now explicitly describe
\Endo^{0}(B_{x})
for each
x
.

Proposition 7.28
.

Suppose
2\notin\Bbbk^{\times}
. The algebra
\Endo^{0}(B_{x})
has a presentation

\displaystyle\Endo^{0}(B_{x})\cong\frac{\Bbbk[\varphi_{E}|E\subseteq E(x)]}{\left(\varphi_{E}\varphi_{E^{\prime}}-2^{|E\cap E^{\prime}|}(-1)^{\ell(E\cap E^{\prime})/2}\varphi_{E\cup E^{\prime}}\right)}.

Proof.

Fix
x\in{}^{I}W
.
If
E(x)=\emptyset
then
\Endo^{0}(B_{x})\cong\Bbbk
so the claim is vacuously true.
Assume that
E(x)\neq\emptyset
.
Proposition
7.9
implies
\varphi_{\{t\}}^{2}=2(-1)^{t/2}\varphi_{\{t\}}
any
t\in E(x)
.
Hence the claim follows from proving the identity

\displaystyle\varphi_{E}=\prod_{t\in E}\varphi_{\{t\}}

holds for any
E\subseteq E(x)
.
If
E=\emptyset
this is vacuously true, so assume
E\neq\emptyset
.
Let
\ux
be the column reduced expression of
x
.
Recall
E=\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{k-1}>t_{k}\}
gives rise to a decomposition

\displaystyle\ux=\ur_{1}\uu_{t_{1}}\ur_{2}\dots\uu_{t_{k-1}}\ur_{k}\uu_{t_{k}}\ur_{k+1}.

Now let
E^{\prime}=\{t_{1}>\dots>t_{k-1}\}
, i.e. omit the final term.
There are two cases to consider.

Case 1
(
\ur_{k}\neq\emptyset
):
Note that
\varphi_{E^{\prime}}=f\otimes\id_{\ur_{k}\uu_{t_{k}}\ur_{k+1}}
where
f
is the double-light-leaf obtained by truncating the subexpression
\ux^{E}
to only include the terms
\ur_{1}\uu_{t_{1}}\ur_{2}\dots\uu_{t_{k-1}}
.
Since
\ur_{k}\neq\emptyset
, we also have
\varphi_{\{t_{k}\}}=\id_{\ur_{1}\uu_{t_{1}}\ur_{2}\dots\uu_{t_{k-1}}}\otimes g
where
g
is the double light leaf obtained from the subexpression
\ur_{k}^{1}\uu_{t_{k}}^{0}\ur_{k+1}^{1}
.
Note
g
is well-defined as the trivalent vertices associated to the even reflections in
\uu_{t_{k}}^{0}
are formed with identity strands in
\ur_{k}^{1}
.
Hence
\varphi_{E^{\prime}}\varphi_{\{t_{k}\}}=f\otimes g=\varphi_{E}
.
As an illustrative example:

where the top-left rectangle represents
f
and the bottom-right rectangle represents
g
.

Case 2
(
\ur_{k}=\emptyset
):
As explained in Section
7.3
, if
\ur_{k}=\emptyset
then the even reflections in
\uu_{t_{k}}
form trivalent vertices with the even reflections ending
\uu_{t_{k-1}}
in the light leaf
LL_{\ux,\{t_{k}\}}
.
Consequently the left-most even reflections in
\varphi_{\{t_{k}\}}
which form trivalent vertices have a non-trivial interaction with the right-most even reflections forming trivalent vertices in
\varphi_{E^{\prime}}
.
This is clearly the only interaction which is not resolved by isotopy relations.
Note that for any
s
-reflection, repeatedly applying Frobenius associativity shows

The general case where
\ur_{j}=\emptyset
, for all
l\leq j\leq k
, follows by repeatedly applying Frobenius associativity in a similar fashion. ∎

Proposition 7.29
.

Let
2\in\Bbbk^{\times}
.
For any
x\in{}^{I}W
the element

\displaystyle e_{x}=\sum_{E\subseteq E(x)}\frac{(-1)^{\ell(E)/2}}{(-2)^{|E|}}\varphi_{E}=\prod_{t\in E(x)}\left(1-\frac{(-1)^{t/2}}{2}\varphi_{\{t\}}\right).

is the primitive idempotent in
\Endo^{0}(B_{\ux})
corresponding to the projection
B_{\ux}\twoheadrightarrow B_{x}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.20
,
Proposition
7.28
also provides a presentation for
\Endo^{0}(B_{\ux})
over any complete local ring
\Bbbk
, and for any reduced expression
\ux
of
x
.
The factorised form of
e_{x}
shows
\varphi_{\{t\}}e_{x}=0
for every
t\in E(x)
, and
e_{x}
is idempotent.
Moreover, it is evident from the presentation that
\{\varphi_{\{t\}}~|~t\in E(x)\}
generates the unique maximal ideal of
\Endo^{0}(B_{x})
.
Hence
e_{x}
annihilates every non-identity morphism.
Consequently
e_{x}
must be the projection
B_{\ux}\twoheadrightarrow B_{x}
, as
[
LW
, §6]
implies
\Endo^{0}(B_{x})
is spanned by the identity morphism.
∎

This gives a third proof that the
{}^{p}d_{x}=d_{x}
when
p>2
.

Example 7.30
.

Continuing from Example
7.25
where
x=\{6,5,4,3,2,1\}
, the idempotent is

\displaystyle e_{x}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{\{2\}}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{\{4\}}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{\{6\}}\right)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{\{2\}}-\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{\{4\}}+\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{\{6\}}-\frac{1}{4}\varphi_{\{4,2\}}+\frac{1}{4}\varphi_{\{6,2\}}-\frac{1}{4}\varphi_{\{6,4\}}-\frac{1}{8}\varphi_{\{6,4,2\}}.

Remark 7.31
.

Essentially identical arguments show that when
\h
is a Cartan realisation of type
\tC_{n}
and
\ux
is any reduced expression of
x\in{}^{I}W
, the algebra
\Endo^{0}(B_{\ux})
has a presentation

\displaystyle\Endo^{0}(B_{\ux})\cong\frac{\Bbbk[\varphi_{E}|E\subseteq E(x)]}{\left(\varphi_{E}\varphi_{E^{\prime}}-(-1)^{\ell(E\cap E^{\prime})/2}\varphi_{E\cup E^{\prime}}\right)}.

One can then argue as in Proposition
7.29
to show

\displaystyle e_{x}=\sum_{E\subseteq E(x)}(-1)^{\ell(E)/2+|E|}\varphi_{E}=\prod_{t\in E(x)}\left(1-(-1)^{t/2}\varphi_{\{t\}}\right)

is the primitive idempotent corresponding to the projection
B_{\ux}\rightarrow B_{x}
.
Since
e_{x}
is defined over
\Z
, this a third proof
{}^{p}d_{x}=d_{x}
for all
p
.

Example 7.32
.

If we
x=\{6,5,4,3,2,1\}
when
\h
is a Cartan realisation of type
\tC_{n}
, the basis of
\Endo^{0}(B_{\ux})
is identical to that in Example
7.25
, but the expression for the idempotent becomes

\displaystyle e_{x}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\varphi_{\{2\}}\right)\left(1+\varphi_{\{4\}}\right)\left(1-\varphi_{\{6\}}\right)

\displaystyle=1-\varphi_{\{2\}}+\varphi_{\{4\}}-\varphi_{\{6\}}-\varphi_{\{4,2\}}+\varphi_{\{6,2\}}-\varphi_{\{6,4\}}+\varphi_{\{6,4,2\}}.

7.8
Antispherical categories for odd dimensional quadric hypersurfaces

We conclude by determining the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases of antispherical categories for odd dimensional quadric hypersurfaces.
Throughout we take
\h
to be a Cartan realisation of type
\tC_{n}
, and
I\subseteq S
will be type
\tC_{n-1}
.
We label our simple reflections as follows:

0
1
2
n-1
…

There is a unique minimal coset representative
x_{i}
of length
i
in
W^{I}
, where
0\leq i<2n
.
Each minimal coset representative
x_{i}
has a unique reduced expression
\ux_{i}
where

\displaystyle\ux_{i}=\begin{cases}\underline{\emptyset}&\text{ if }i=0,\\
s_{n-1}s_{n-2}\dots s_{n-i}&\text{ if }0<i\leq n,\\
s_{n-1}s_{n-2}\dots s_{1}s_{0}s_{1}s_{2}\dots s_{i-n}&\text{ if }n<i<2n,\end{cases}

A simple induction shows:

Lemma 7.33
.

For
0\leq i<2n
the Bott-Samelson element
d_{\ux_{i}}
is

\displaystyle d_{\ux_{i}}=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{lll}\delta^{I}_{\id}&=d_{x_{i}}&\text{ if }i=0\\
\delta^{I}_{x_{i}}+v\delta^{I}_{x_{i-1}}&=d_{x_{i}}&\text{ if }0<i\leq n,\\
\delta^{I}_{x_{i}}+v\delta^{I}_{x_{i-1}}+\delta^{I}_{x_{2n-i}}+v\delta^{I}_{x_{2n-i-1}}&=d_{x_{i}}+d_{x_{2n-i}}&\text{ if }n<i<2n.\end{array}\end{cases}

It follows immediately from Lemma
2.1
that
{}^{p}d_{x_{i}}=d_{x_{i}}
for all
p
, whenever
0\leq i\leq n
.
When
n<i<2n
, the only local intersection form that needs to be computed is
I_{x_{2n-i},\ux_{i}}
.
Observe that

\displaystyle\ux_{i}^{\ue}:=\begin{smallmatrix}\ua&\ua&\dots&\ua&\ua&\da&\dots&\da&\da\\
1&1&\dots&1&0&1&\dots&1&0\\
s_{n-1}&s_{n-2}&\dots&s_{1}&s_{0}&s_{1}&\dots&s_{i-n-1}&s_{i-n}\end{smallmatrix}\subset\ux_{i}

is an
I
-antispherical subexpression of
\ux_{i}
which evaluates to
x_{2n-i}
and has defect
0
.
Note that the corresponding light leaf
LL_{\ux_{i},e}
is

Hence, the local intersection form is the
1\times 1
matrix

\displaystyle I_{x_{n-i},\ux_{i}}=\left[2(-1)^{n-i}\right].

The following proposition follows immediately.

Proposition 7.34
.

Let
\h
be a Cartan realisation of type
\tC_{n}
and
I\subset S
of type
\tC_{n-1}
.
The
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis of
\DRA(\h,I,\Bbbk)
is

\displaystyle{}^{p}d_{x}=\begin{cases}d_{\ux}&\text{if }2\notin\Bbbk^{\times},\text{ and }\\
d_{x}&\text{if }2\in\Bbbk^{\times},\end{cases}

where
\ux
is the unique reduced expression of
x
.
Moreover, each
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomial is a monic monomial.

Remark 7.35
.

We can again obtain a presentation for the degree-0 endomorphism algebra of Bott-Samelson objects.
If
0\leq i\leq n
then
\Endo^{0}(B_{\ux_{i}})\cong\Bbbk
.
If
n<i<2n
then

\displaystyle\Endo^{0}(B_{\ux_{i}})\cong\frac{\Bbbk[\varphi]}{\varphi^{2}-2(-1)^{n-i}\varphi}.

If
2\in\Bbbk^{\times}
the idempotent
e_{x}
corresponding to the projection
B_{\ux_{i}}\twoheadrightarrow B_{x_{i}}
is
e_{x_{i}}=1-(-1)^{n-i}\varphi/2
, giving a third proof
{}^{p}d_{x}=d_{x}
for all
x\in{}^{I}W
if
p>2
.

Remark 7.36
.

Modifying the preceding arguments shows if
\h
is type
\tB_{n}
and
I
type
\tB_{n-1}
then for
n<i<2n
we have

\displaystyle\Endo^{0}(B_{\ux_{i}})\cong\frac{\Bbbk[\varphi]}{\varphi^{2}-(-1)^{n-i}\varphi}.

The idempotent
e_{x}
corresponding to the projection
B_{\ux_{i}}\twoheadrightarrow B_{x_{i}}
is
e_{x_{i}}=1-(-1)^{n-i}\varphi
which is defined over
\Z
, giving a third proof
{}^{p}d_{x}=d_{x}
for all
p
and all
x\in{}^{I}W
.

8
Graded decomposition numbers

We now relate the graded decomposition numbers for mixed tilting, projective, parity and intersection cohomology sheaves under the (derived) extension of scalars and (derived) modular reduction functors.
This generalises
[
AR1
, §2.7]
to the mixed and parabolic settings.
The results of this Section will be used in Section
9
to determine the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis of spherical (co)minuscule Hecke categories.

8.1
Standard and Costandard objects

We recall basic properties of (co)standard objects following
[
AR1
,
AR2
,
AMRW
]
.

For a Schubert cell
B\backslash Bx\cdot B
, let
i_{x}:B\backslash Bx\cdot B\hookrightarrow B\backslash G
denote the locally closed inclusion of strata.
The functors
i_{x*}
and
i_{x!}
restrict to functors of the subcategories of parity sheaves, so induce functors on the relevant mixed categories.
We define
\Delta_{x}^{B}
and
\nabla_{x}^{B}
in
D^{\mix}(B\backslash G/B)
by

\displaystyle\Delta_{x}^{B}:=i_{x!}\underline{\Bbbk}_{X_{x}}(\ell(x))

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}^{B}:=i_{x*}\underline{\Bbbk}_{X_{x}}(\ell(x))

and denote by
\Delta_{x}^{U}
and
\nabla_{x}^{U}
(resp.
\Delta_{x}^{P}
and
\nabla_{x}^{P}
) the analogously defined sheaves in
D^{\mix}(B\backslash G/U)
(resp.
D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)
).
There is an analogous construction of
\Delta_{x}
and
\nabla_{x}
in
D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)
where the Artin-Schreier sheaf
\mathcal{L}_{\psi}
is used in lieu of
\underline{\Bbbk}
, see
[
AMRW
, §6.2]
.
The objects
\Delta
(resp.
\nabla
) are standard (resp. costandard) objects in the perverse
t
-structures on each of the preceding categories
[
AR2
, §4.3]
.

The categories
D^{\mix}(B\backslash G/B)
,
D^{\mix}(B\backslash G/U)
and
D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)
are related by the functors

\displaystyle\For:D^{\mix}(B\backslash G/B)\longrightarrow D^{\mix}(B\backslash G/U),

\displaystyle Av:D^{\mix}(B\backslash G/U)\longrightarrow D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G),

where
\For
is the functor forgetting
B
-equivariance and
Av
was introduced in Section
4.3
.
The former is
t
-exact and satisfies
\For(\Delta_{x}^{B})\cong\Delta_{x}^{U}
for all
x\in W

[
AR2
, §3.5]
.
The latter satisfies
Av(\Delta_{x}^{U})\cong\Delta_{x}
for all
x\in W^{I}
by
[
AMRW
, §6.2]
.
Analogous statements hold for each
\nabla_{x}^{B}
,
\nabla_{x}^{U}
and
\nabla_{x}
.
Moreover,
\For
and
Av
each commute with the action (induced by convolution) of
D^{\mix}(B\backslash G/B)
on
D^{\mix}(B\backslash G/U)
and
D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)
respectively, see
[
AR2
]
and
[
AMRW
, §6.2]
.
We denote this action by
\ustar
.

We note the following property of
\nabla_{x}^{U}
.

Lemma 8.1
.

For any
x\in W^{I}
, we have
Av(\nabla_{xw_{I}}^{U})\cong\nabla_{x}\langle\ell(w_{I})\rangle
.

Proof.

For any
s\in S
,
\nabla_{s}^{B}
is represented by a complex with
\cE_{\id}(-1)
in cohomological degree
-1
, and
\cE_{s}
in cohomological degree
0
.
Recall
\nabla_{ys}^{B}\cong\nabla_{y}^{B}\ustar\nabla_{s}^{B}
whenever
xs>x

[
AR2
, §4.3]
.
Basic Kazhdan-Lusztig cell theory implies
\langle\cE_{y}|y\neq\id\rangle_{\oplus,(1)}
forms a tensor ideal in
\Parity(B\backslash G/B)
.
Hence
\nabla_{w_{I}}^{B}
is represented by a complex with a unique summand
\cE_{\id}
in cohomological degree
-\ell(w_{I})
and internal degree
-\ell(w_{I})
.
The claim follows from
\For(\nabla_{x}^{B})\cong\nabla_{x}^{U}
as in
[
AR2
, §3.5]
,
Av
satisfying
Av(\cE_{y})\cong 0
if
y\notin w^{I}

[
RW1
, §11.2]
and
\ustar
commuting with
\For
and
Av

[
AMRW
, §6.2]
.
∎

8.2
Mixed parabolic Ringel duality

We now construct mixed, parabolic Ringel duality functors.
Throughout we require
\Bbbk
is a field.

Recall Ringel duality for
D^{\mix}(B\backslash G/U)
is an autoequivalence
\tR
satisfying
\tR(T_{x})\cong P_{w_{0}x}
.
In
[
AR2
, §4.4]
it is defined as
\tR(A)\cong\Delta_{w_{0}}^{B}\ustar A
.
An essential property of their construction is
\tR(\nabla_{x}^{U})\cong\Delta_{w_{0}x}^{U}
, which categorifies
\delta_{w_{0}}\overline{\delta_{x}}=\delta_{w_{0}x}
in the Hecke algebra.
At the level of Bruhat orders, this descends to the antiinvolution
x\mapsto w_{0}x
on
W
.
The parabolic analogue is the antiinvolution
x\mapsto w_{0}xw_{I}
on
W^{I}
, and for any
x\in W^{I}
we have the identity in
N^{I}

\displaystyle(-v^{-1})^{\ell(w_{I})}\,\delta_{w_{0}}\cdot(\overline{\delta_{x}}\otimes 1)=\delta_{w_{0}}\overline{\delta_{x}}\otimes(-v^{-1})^{\ell(w_{I})}=\delta_{w_{0}}\overline{\delta_{x}}\,\overline{\delta_{w_{I}}}\otimes 1=\delta_{w_{0}xw_{I}}\otimes 1.

Motivated by this identity we define

\displaystyle\tR_{A}:D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)\longrightarrow D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)~~\text{ where }~~C\mapsto\Delta_{w_{0}}^{B}\langle\ell(w_{I})\rangle\ustar C.

Lemma 8.2
.

For any
x\in W^{I}
we have
\tR_{A}(\nabla_{x})\cong\Delta_{w_{0}xw_{I}}
.

Proof.

The claim follows from Lemma
8.1
and the fact
Av
and
\For
commute with
\ustar
, see
[
AMRW
, §6.2]
.
In particular, for any
x\in W^{I}
we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{w_{0}}^{B}\langle\ell(w_{I})\rangle\ustar\nabla_{x}

\displaystyle\cong\Delta_{w_{0}}^{B}\ustar\nabla_{x}\langle\ell(w_{I})\rangle

\displaystyle\cong\Delta_{w_{0}}^{B}\ustar\left(Av\circ\For(\nabla_{xw_{I}}^{B})\right)

\displaystyle\cong Av\circ\For(\Delta_{w_{0}}^{B}\ustar\nabla_{xw_{I}}^{B})

\displaystyle\cong Av\circ\For(\Delta_{w_{0}xw_{I}}^{B})

\displaystyle\cong\Delta_{w_{0}xw_{I}},

which proves the claim.
∎

Proposition 8.3
.

The functor
\tR_{A}:D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)\rightarrow D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)
is an equivalence of categories with quasi-inverse
\tR_{A}^{-1}(C)=\nabla_{w_{0}}^{B}\langle-\ell(w_{I})\rangle\ustar C
.
Moreover,
\tR_{A}
satisfies
\tR_{A}(T_{x})\cong P_{w_{0}xw_{I}}
for each
x\in W^{I}
.

Our proof follows the arguments of
[
BBM
, §2.3]
,
[
Yun
, §4.2.1]
and
[
ARV
, §10.2]
.

Proof.

That
\tR_{A}
is an equivalence with quasi-inverse
\tR_{A}^{-1}
is immediate from
\Delta_{x}^{B}\ustar\nabla_{x}^{B}\cong\underline{\Bbbk}_{X_{\id}}

[
AR1
, §4.3]
and the fact that
\ustar
commutes with
\For
and
Av
.
It remains to show
\tR_{A}
has the desired effect on tilting objects.

Fix
x\in W^{I}
, let
T_{x}
denote the corresponding indecomposable tilting and set
P=\tR_{A}(T_{x})
.
Lemma
8.2
and the fact
T_{x}
is tilting imply
P
is perverse and has a
\Delta
-filtration.
It is shown in
[
AMRW
, §6.2]
that

\displaystyle\Hom_{D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)}(\Delta_{x},\nabla_{y}\langle n\rangle[m])=\begin{cases}\Bbbk&\text{if }x=y\text{ and }n=m=0,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Hence,

\displaystyle\Hom_{D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)}(P,\Delta_{y}\langle n\rangle[m])\cong\Hom_{D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)}(T_{x},\nabla_{w_{0}yw_{I}}\langle n\rangle[m])

can only be non-zero when
m=0
.
Since
D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)^{\leq 0}
is generated under extensions by
\Delta_{x}\langle m\rangle[n]
, where
x\in W
,
m\in\Z
and
n\in\Z_{\geq 0}
, it follows for any
C\in D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)^{\leq 0}
that

\displaystyle\Hom_{D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)}(P,C)=0

and consequently, for any
C^{\prime}
in
\Pmixw(B\backslash G)
we have

\displaystyle\text{Ext}^{1}_{\Pmixw(B\backslash G)}(P,C^{\prime})=0.

Hence
P
is projective.
Since
\tR_{A}
is an equivalence and
T_{x}
indecomposable,
\Endo(P)\cong\Endo(T_{x})
is a local ring, so
P
is indecomposable.
Finally, the kernel of
T_{x}\twoheadrightarrow\nabla_{x}
admits a
\nabla
-filtration.
Hence we obtain a surjections
P\twoheadrightarrow\nabla_{w_{0}xw_{I}}\twoheadrightarrow L_{w_{0}xw_{I}}
.
Thus,
P\cong P_{w_{0}xw_{I}}
.
∎

We now construct mixed Ringel duality for
D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)
.
Since
D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)
is not a module category for
D^{\mix}(B\backslash G/B)
our construction is less direct than desired.

Proposition 8.4
.

Suppose
2\in\Bbbk^{\times}
. Define
\tR_{S}:D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)\rightarrow D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)
as the composition
R_{S}=\kappa\circ\tR_{A}\circ\kappa
.
Then
\tR_{S}(T_{x})\cong P_{w_{0}xw_{I}}
for any
x\in W^{I}
, and
\tR_{S}
has quasi-inverse
\tR_{S}^{-1}=\kappa\circ\tR_{A}^{-1}\circ\kappa
.

Proof.

Parabolic Koszul duality and Lemma
8.2
imply
\tR_{S}(\nabla_{x}^{P})\cong\Delta_{w_{0}xw_{I}}^{P}
for all
x\in W^{I}
.
The claim then follows from arguments similar to that in Proposition
8.3
.
∎

Remark 8.5
.

A more direct construction using the ‘free monodromic category’ of
[
AMRW
, §2.3]
may be possible.
However, it is currently unknown if the free monodromic analogue of
\Delta_{w_{0}}
is convolutive.

8.3
Extension of scalars and modular reduction

We now recall collect facts regarding the behaviour of various sheaves under extension of scalars and modular reduction.
These results can all be found in either
[
AR2
]
or
[
AMRW
]
.

Fix a
p
-modular system
(\bO,\bK,\bF)
.
For
\Bbbk\in\{\bO,\bK,\bF\}
we let
{}^{\Bbbk}\check{\cE}_{x}
denote the indecomposable parity sheaf in
D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(\check{B}\backslash\check{G},\Bbbk)
, we take
{}^{\Bbbk}\cE_{x},{}^{\Bbbk}T_{x},{}^{\Bbbk}P_{x},{}^{\Bbbk}L_{x},{}^{\Bbbk}\Delta_{x},{}^{\Bbbk}\nabla_{x}
to be the usual objects in
D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)
.
Note that the existence of these objects when
\Bbbk=\bO
is shown in
[
AR2
]
.
In Section
8.4
we will also consider the non-mixed indecomposable tilting
{}^{\Bbbk}\cT_{x}
, projective
{}^{\Bbbk}\cP_{x}
, and simple intersection cohomology
\mathcal{IC}_{x}
sheaves in
D^{b}(U\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)
, and the corresponding sheaves
{}^{\Bbbk}\cT_{x}^{B}
,
{}^{\Bbbk}\cP_{x}^{B}
, and
\mathcal{IC}_{x}^{B}
in
D^{b}(U\backslash G/B,\Bbbk)
.

For mixed sheaves with coefficients in
\bO
, we consider the functors

\displaystyle\bK(-):D_{Wh,I}^{\mix}(B\backslash G,\bO)\rightarrow D_{Wh,I}^{\mix}(B\backslash G,\bK)

\displaystyle\bF(-):D_{Wh,I}^{\mix}(B\backslash G,\bO)\rightarrow D_{Wh,I}^{\mix}(B\backslash G,\bF)

\displaystyle\bK(-):D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bO)\rightarrow D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bK)

\displaystyle\bF(-):D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bO)\rightarrow D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bF)

each of which is induced by the derived extension of scalars functors.
These functors commute with Koszul duality by
[
AR2
, §5.3]
.

Our interest lies in how various sheaves behave under extension of scalars
\bK(-)
and modular reduction
\bF(-)
.
Standard and costandard objects are well-behaved under both functors

\displaystyle\bF({}^{\bO}\Delta_{x})\cong{}^{\bF}\Delta_{x}

\displaystyle\bF({}^{\bO}\nabla_{x})\cong{}^{\bF}\nabla_{x}

\displaystyle\bK({}^{\bO}\Delta_{x})\cong{}^{\bK}\Delta_{x}

\displaystyle\bK({}^{\bO}\nabla_{x})\cong{}^{\bK}\nabla_{x}

by
[
AR2
, §3.1]
.
Parity, tilting and projective sheaves remain indecomposable under modular reduction

\displaystyle\bF({}^{\bO}\check{\cE}_{x})\cong{}^{\bF}\check{\cE}_{x}

\displaystyle\bF({}^{\bO}\cE_{x})\cong{}^{\bF}\cE_{x}

\displaystyle\bF({}^{\bO}T_{x})\cong{}^{\bF}T_{x}

\displaystyle\bF({}^{\bO}P_{x})\cong{}^{\bF}P_{x}

by
[
AR2
, §3.1]
.
While extension of scalars
\bK(-)
takes parity sheaves to parity sheaves
[
JMW
, §2.5]
, mixed tilting sheaves to mixed tilting sheave, and mixed projective sheaves to mixed projective sheaves
[
AR2
, §3.3]
.
In particular, we define integers
\check{e}_{y,x}^{i}
,
e_{y,x}^{i}
,
t_{y,x}^{i}
and
p_{y,x}^{i}
such that

\displaystyle\bK({}^{\bO}\check{\cE}_{x})

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{i\in\Z,y\in W^{I}}{}^{\bK}\check{\cE}_{x}(i)^{\oplus\check{e}_{y,x}^{i}},

\displaystyle\bK({}^{\bO}\cE_{x})

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{i\in\Z,y\in W^{I}}{}^{\bK}\cE_{x}(i)^{\oplus e_{y,x}^{i}},

\displaystyle\bK({}^{\bO}T_{x})

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{i\in\Z,y\in W^{I}}{}^{\bK}T_{x}\langle i\rangle^{\oplus t_{y,x}^{i}},

\displaystyle\bK({}^{\bO}P_{x})

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{i\in\Z,y\in W^{I}}{}^{\bK}P_{x}\langle i\rangle^{\oplus p_{y,x}^{i}}.

Finally, in
[
AR2
, §3.1]
it is shown that
\bK({}^{\bO}L_{x})\cong{}^{\bK}L_{x}
and
\bF({}^{\bO}L_{x})
is perverse.
Since
P^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)
is finite-length, the Jordan-Hölder multiplicities
l_{y,x}^{i}:=[\bF({}^{\bO}L_{x}):{}^{\bF}L_{y}\langle i\rangle]
are well defined.

8.4
Graded decomposition numbers

In Section
8.3
we introduced the graded decomposition numbers
\check{e}_{y,x}^{i}
,
t_{y,x}^{i}
,
p_{y,x}^{i}
and
l_{y,x}^{i}
.
We now relate these decomposition numbers.
Define Laurent polynomials

\displaystyle\check{e}_{y,x}^{\mix}=\sum_{i\in\Z}\check{e}_{y,x}^{i}v^{i},

\displaystyle t_{y,x}^{\mix}=\sum_{i\in\Z}t_{y,x}^{i}v^{i},

\displaystyle p_{y,x}^{\mix}=\sum_{i\in\Z}p_{y,x}^{i}v^{i},

\displaystyle l_{y,x}^{\mix}=\sum_{i\in\Z}l_{y,x}^{i}v^{i},

and consider the matrices

\displaystyle\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{I}^{\mix}=(\check{e}_{y,x}^{\mix}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}_{I}^{\mix}=(t_{y,x}^{\mix}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{I}^{\mix}=(p_{y,x}^{\mix}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{I}^{\mix}=(l_{y,x}^{\mix}),

where
y,x\in W^{I}
.
Given any matrix
\mathfrak{M}=(m_{y,x})
where
y,x\in W^{I}
we define
\tR\mathfrak{M}
to be the matrix
\tR\mathfrak{M}=(m_{w_{0}yw_{I},w_{0}xw_{I}})
, and
\mathfrak{M}^{t}
to be the transpose matrix.

The following relationship between the decomposition numbers is a direct generalisation of
[
AR1
, §2.7]
to the mixed and parabolic settings.

Proposition 8.6
.

For any
I
and
p>2
we have

\displaystyle\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{I}^{\mix}=\mathfrak{T}_{I}^{\mix}=\tR\mathfrak{P}_{I}^{\mix}=\tR\mathfrak{L}_{I}^{\mix,t}.

We prove the Proposition in the following lemmata.

Lemma 8.7
.

For any
I\subseteq S
and
p>2
, we have
\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{I}^{\mix}=\mathfrak{T}_{I}^{\mix}
.

Proof.

Extension of scalars gives rise to the identity

\displaystyle\ch[{}^{\bO}T_{x}]=\sum_{i\in\Z,y\in W^{I}}t_{y,x}^{i}[{}^{\bK}T_{y}](-v^{-1})^{i}.

On the other hand, parabolic Koszul duality and extension of scalars shows

\displaystyle\ch[{}^{\bO}T_{x}]=\kappa(\ch[{}^{\bO}\check{\cE}_{x}])=\kappa\left(\sum_{i\in\Z,y\in W^{I}}\check{e}_{y,x}^{i}\ch[{}^{\bK}\check{\cE}_{y}]v^{i}\right)=\sum_{i\in\Z,y\in W^{I}}\check{e}_{y,x}^{i}\ch[{}^{\bK}T_{y}](-v^{-1})^{i}.

Equating coefficients gives
t_{y,x}^{i}=\check{e}_{y,x}^{i}
which proves the claim.
∎

Lemma 8.8
.

For any
I\subseteq S
and
p>2
, we have
\mathfrak{T}_{I}^{\mix}=\tR\mathfrak{P}_{I}^{\mix}
.

Proof.

First, note that mixed spherical Ringel duality
\tR_{S}
implies

\displaystyle\ch[{}^{\bF}P_{w_{0}yw_{I}}]=v^{\ell(w_{I})}\delta_{w_{0}}\ch[{}^{\bF}T_{x}],

\displaystyle\ch[{}^{\bK}P_{w_{0}yw_{I}}]=v^{\ell(w_{I})}\delta_{w_{0}}\ch[{}^{\bK}T_{x}].

Modular reduction gives
\ch[{}^{\bF}T_{x}]=\ch[{}^{\bO}T_{x}]
and
\ch[{}^{\bF}P_{x}]=\ch[{}^{\bO}P_{x}]
.
Combining these facts, we obtain

\displaystyle\ch[{}^{\bO}P_{w_{0}xw_{I}}]

\displaystyle=(-v^{-1})^{\ell(w_{I})}\delta_{w_{0}}\ch[{}^{\bO}T_{x}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\Z,y\in W^{I}}t_{y,x}^{i}(-v^{-1})^{\ell(w_{I})}\delta_{w_{0}}\ch[{}^{\bK}T_{y}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\Z,y\in W^{I}}t_{y,x}^{i}\ch[{}^{\bK}P_{w_{0}yw_{I}}].

Comparing coefficients with
\ch[\bK({}^{\bO}P_{w_{0}xw_{I}})]
proves the claim.
∎

Lemma 8.9
.

For any
I\subseteq S
, we have
\mathfrak{R}_{I}^{\mix}=\mathfrak{L}_{I}^{\mix,t}
.

This argument is a modification of that in
[
AR1
, §2.7]
.

Proof.

The arguments of
[
RSW
, §2.4]
and
[
AR1
, §5.2]
appply in the mixed setting.
In particular, for any
x,y\in W^{I}
and
i\in\Z
the space
\Hom_{D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bO)}({}^{\bO}P_{x}\langle i\rangle,{}^{\bO}L_{y})
is a free
\bO
-module, and we have isomorphisms of
\bF
-vector spaces

\displaystyle\bF\otimes_{\bO}\Hom_{D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bO)}({}^{\bO}P_{x}\langle i\rangle,{}^{\bO}L_{y})\cong\Hom_{D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bF)}({}^{\bF}P_{x}\langle i\rangle,\bF({}^{\bO}L_{y})).

Hence
\rank_{\bO}\Hom_{D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bO)}({}^{\bO}P_{x}\langle i\rangle,{}^{\bO}L_{y})=[\bF({}^{\bO}L_{y}):{}^{\bF}L_{x}\langle i\rangle]
.
On the other hand, we have isomorphisms

\displaystyle\bK\otimes_{\bO}\Hom_{D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bO)}({}^{\bO}P_{x}\langle i\rangle,{}^{\bO}L_{y})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bK)}(\bK({}^{\bO}P_{x}),{}^{\bK}L_{y}\langle-i\rangle)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bK)}\left(\bigoplus_{j\in\Z,z\in W^{I}}{}^{\bK}P_{z}\langle j\rangle^{\oplus p_{z,x}^{j}},{}^{\bK}L_{y}\langle-i\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bK)}({}^{\bK}P_{y}\langle-i\rangle^{\oplus p_{y,x}^{-i}},{}^{\bK}L_{y}\langle-i\rangle).

Hence
\rank_{\bO}\Hom_{D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bO)}({}^{\bO}P_{x}\langle i\rangle,{}^{\bO}L_{y})=p_{y,x}^{-i}
.
The self-duality of parity sheaves
[
JMW
, §2.2]
together with Lemmata
8.7
and
8.8
imply
p_{y,x}^{-i}=p_{y,x}^{i}
.
Hence the claim.
∎

Let
\mathfrak{T}_{I}=(t_{y,x})
,
\mathfrak{P}_{I}=(p_{y,x})
and
\mathfrak{L}_{I}=(l_{y,x})
denote the corresponding matrices of decomposition numbers for the (unmixed) sheaves
{}^{\bO}\cT_{x}
,
{}^{\bO}\cP_{x}
and
{}^{\bO}\mathcal{IC}_{x}
in
D^{b}(U\backslash G/P)
respectively.
We have the following (unmixed) analogue of
[
AR1
, §2.7]
.

Lemma 8.10
.

For any
I\subseteq S
and any
p\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}_{I}=\tR\mathfrak{P}_{I}=\tR\mathfrak{L}_{I}^{t}.

The following sketches how the argument in
[
AR1
, §2.7]
can be extended to the parabolic setting.

Proof.

In
[
AR1
, §2.3]
(unmixed) Ringel duality is constructed using the Radon transform for flag varieties.
In
[
Yun
, §5.4]
a Radon transform is constructed for partial flag varieties, and the arguments of
[
AR1
, §B.5]
continue to hold in this setting.
So
\mathfrak{T}_{I}=\tR\mathfrak{P}_{I}
follows from the geometric construction of Ringel duality and its compatibility with extension of scalars.
Finally,
\mathfrak{P}_{I}=\mathfrak{L}_{I}^{t}
by arguments identical to those in
[
AR1
, §2.7]
.
∎

Given some matrix
\mathfrak{M}
with entries in
\Z[v,v^{-1}]
, we denote by
\mathfrak{M}|_{v=1}
the matrix obtained by the evaluation
v=1
.

Lemma 8.11
.

For any
I\subseteq S
and any good prime
p
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}_{I}^{\mix}|_{v=1}=\mathfrak{T}_{I}=\tR\mathfrak{P}_{I}=\tR\mathfrak{L}_{I}^{t}.

Proof.

Denote by
t_{y,x}^{i,B}
the graded decomposition numbers for
\bK({}^{\bO}T_{x}^{B})
and
t_{y,x}^{B}
the (ungraded) decomposition numbers for
\bK({}^{\bO}\cT_{x}^{B})
.
Since
p
is a good prime for
G
, we can apply the degrading functor of
[
AR2
, §5.3]
.
Namely, there is a
t
-exact functor
\mu:D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/B)\rightarrow D^{b}(U\backslash G/B)
satisfying (among other things)
\mu\circ\langle 1\rangle\cong\mu
and
\mu(T_{x}^{B})\cong\cT_{x}^{B}
for each
x\in W
.
Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle t_{y,x}^{B}=\sum_{i}t_{y,x}^{i,B}.

The natural map
p:G/B\rightarrow G/P
induces functors on
p_{*}:D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/B)\rightarrow D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)
and
p_{*}:D^{b}(U\backslash G/B)\rightarrow D^{b}(U\backslash G/P)
satisfying

\displaystyle p_{*}(T_{x}^{B})\cong\begin{cases}T_{x}&\text{if }x\in W^{I},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle p_{*}(\cT_{x}^{B})\cong\begin{cases}\cT_{x}&\text{if }x\in W^{I},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

respectively, by
[
AMRW
, §6.3]
and
[
Yun
, §3.4]
.
These imply that for any
x,y\in W^{I}
we have

\displaystyle t_{y,x}=t_{y,x}^{B}=\sum_{i\in\Z}t_{y,x}^{i,B}=\sum_{i\in\Z}t_{y,x}^{i}=t_{y,x}^{\mix}|_{v=1}

where
t_{y,x}
denotes the relevant decomposition number for
\bK({}^{\bO}\cT_{x})
.
Hence
\mathfrak{T}_{I}^{\mix}|_{v=1}=\mathfrak{T}_{I}
.
The remaining equalities follow from Lemma
8.10
.
∎

Recall that
e_{y,x}^{i}
denotes the relevant decomposition number for
\bK({}^{\bO}\cE_{x})
in
D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\bK)
.
Define the Laurent polynomial
e^{\mix}_{y,x}
and the matrix
\mathfrak{E}
by

\displaystyle e^{\mix}_{y,x}=\sum_{i}e_{y,x}^{i}v^{i},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{I}=(e^{\mix}_{y,x})

respectively.
Note these should not to be confused with
\check{e}^{\mix}_{y,x}
and
\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{I}
which encode the decomposition numbers for parity objects in
D^{\mix}_{Wh,I}(B\backslash G)
.

We will need the following parabolic analogue of
[
Wil1
, §2.2]
.

Lemma 8.12
.

Fix
x\in W^{I}
and suppose
y<x
is maximal in the Bruhat order such that
e_{y,x}\neq 0
. If
e_{y,x}\in\Z
then
l_{y,x}=e_{x,y}
.

Proof.

The arguments in
[
Wil1
, §2.2]
hold if one instead uses
X=\bigsqcup_{z\geq x}B\cdot xP/P
,
Z=B\cdot xB
and
U=X\backslash Z
.
9
9

9

There is a typographic error in
[
Wil1
, §2.2]
:
U=X\backslash U
should be
U=X\backslash Z
.

∎

Proposition 8.13
.

Let
I\subseteq S
be arbitrary and
p>2
be a good prime for
G
.
If
\check{\mathfrak{E}}
is the identity matrix, then
\check{\mathfrak{E}}=\mathfrak{E}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
8.6
, Lemma
8.11
and the assumptions on
p
, we have
\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{I}^{\mix}|_{v=1}=\mathfrak{L}_{I}
.
Hence
\mathfrak{L}_{I}
is the identity matrix.
Now, suppose for a contradiction that
x\in W^{I}
is minimal with respect to the Bruhat order such that
\bK({}^{\bO}\cE_{x})\not\cong{}^{\bK}\cE_{x}
.
Denote by
{}^{\bO,B}\cE_{x}
the corresponding indecomposable sheaf in
D^{\mix}(B\backslash G/P,\bO)
and note that
{}^{\bO}\cE_{x}
is the image of
{}^{\bO,B}\cE_{x}
under the functor forgetting
B
-equivariance,
[
JMW
, §4.1]
.
Then, for any
s\in S
such that
sx<x
, we have

\displaystyle\bK({}^{\bO}\cE_{s}^{B}\star{}^{\bO,B}\cE_{sx})\cong{}^{\bK}\cE_{s}^{B}\star{}^{\bK,B}\cE_{sx}\cong{}^{\bK,B}\cE_{x}\oplus\bigoplus_{sy\leq y<x}{}^{\bK,B}\cE_{y}^{\oplus\mu_{y,x}^{m}}

where
sy\leq y
means either
sy<y
or
sy\notin W^{I}
, and
\mu_{y,x}^{m}
denotes the coefficient of
v
in the spherical Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomial
m_{y,x}
.
In particular, all summands in the decomposition are in degree 0.
Consequently every summand of
\bK({}^{\bO}\cE_{x})
must also be in degree 0, i.e.
e_{y,x}\in\Z
.
Hence we can apply Lemma
8.12
to obtain a non-zero
l_{y,x}
, contradicting the fact
\mathfrak{L}
is the identity matrix.
∎

Remark 8.14
.

If
\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{I}
is not the identity matrix then the relationship between
\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{I}
and
\mathfrak{E}_{I}
is highly opaque.
For example, if
G=SO_{5}
,
P
is a parabolic of type
\tA_{2}
, and
p=2
then
\mathfrak{E}
has entries in
\{0,1\}
, while
\check{\mathfrak{E}}
has entries in
\Z[v,v^{-1}]
.

9
Spherical (co)minuscule Hecke categories

We now deduce the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis of the spherical (co)minuscule Hecke categories from our knowledge of the antispherical categories and decomposition numbers.

Theorem 9.1
.

Suppose
\Bbbk
is a field or a complete local ring in a
p
-modular system,
D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P,\Bbbk)
is (co)minuscule, and all primes which are not good for
G
are invertible in
\Bbbk
.
The
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig basis of
D^{\mix}(U\backslash G/P)
satisfies
{}^{p}c_{x}=c_{x}
for all
x\in W^{I}
.

Proof.

If
p>2
then the claim is immediate from Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, and Proposition
8.13
.
The only remaining case is
p=2
in type
\tA
.
However, (co)minuscule flag varieties in type
\tA
are all Grassmannians, for which it is known that each Schubert variety
X\subseteq G/P
admits a small resolution
\pi:\tilde{X}\rightarrow X
, see
[
Zel
]
.
In particular
\pi_{*}\underline{\Bbbk}_{\tilde{X}}
is a simple intersection cohomology sheaf, so it is also an indecomposable parity sheaf.
Hence
{}^{p}c_{x}=c_{x}
for all
x\in W^{I}
.
∎

When all primes which are not good for
G
are not invertible in
\Bbbk
we may have
{}^{p}c_{x}\neq c_{x}
.
The most interesting behaviour arises from the Lagrangian Grassmannian.
The elements in
W^{I}
can be identified with subsets of
\{1,\dots,n\}
using the map in Section
7.1
and inversion in
W
.
For each
x\in{}^{I}W
, define the set

\displaystyle F(x):=\{y\subseteq x~|~\text{if }i,i-1\in x\text{ and }i\notin y\text{ then }i-1\notin y\}.

Further, for each
s\in S
and
x\in W^{I}
define
{}^{p}\mu_{s,x}^{y}\in\Z[v,v^{-1}]
by the equality

\displaystyle b_{s}~{}^{p}c_{x}=\sum_{y\in W}{}^{p}\mu_{s,x}^{y}~{}^{p}c_{y}

in
M^{I}
.
We propose the following combinatorial algorithm for determining
{}^{2}c_{x}
for the Lagrangian Grassmannian; i.e. when
\h
is a Cartan realisation of type
\tC_{n}
and
I\subseteq S
is type
\tA_{n-1}
.

Conjecture 9.2
.

Fix
x\in W
and let
i>0
. If
s_{i}x<x
, then

\displaystyle{}^{2}c_{x}=b_{s_{i}}\,{}^{2}c_{s_{i}x}-\sum_{y\notin F(x)}{}^{2}\mu^{y}_{s_{i},s_{i}x}~{}^{2}c_{y}.

Remark 9.3
.

If the left descent set of
x
is
\{s_{0}\}
then Equation
1
implies
x=\{k,\dots,1\}
for some
k\leq n
, and the corresponding Schubert variety is smooth, by
[
KP
, §1]
.
Hence
{}^{2}c_{\{k,\dots,1\}}=c_{\{k,\dots,1\}}
.
For all other
x\in W^{I}
the element
{}^{2}c_{x}
can be computed using Conjecture
9.2
.

Remark 9.4
.

Conjecture
9.2
has been checked up to rank 7 using the IHecke code of
[
GJW
]
.

Remark 9.5
.

In general, one does not expect a combinatorial algorithm will compute
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases.
However, it is clear (only after the fact) from Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
that
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases for antispherical (co)minuscule Hecke categories can be computed using a combinatorial algorithm; they either arise from the Kazhdan-Lusztig algorithm, or they are monomials in the Kazhdan-Lusztig generators.
Thus, one might hope that a combinatorial algorithm also computes the
p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig bases for spherical (co)minuscule Hecke categories.

10
p
-tightness and
p
-small resolutions

We conclude by introducing the notions of
p
-tight elements for a realisation and
p
-small resolutions of singularities.
This is done with a view towards providing a geometric explanation for the characteristic-
2
phenomena in antispherical cominuscule Hecke categories.

10.1
Definitions and basic properties

Let
\Bbbk
a field of characteristic
p
, or a complete local ring with residue field of characteristic
p
.

Fix a realisation
\h
of a Coxeter system
(W,S)
, and denote by
\sD_{\text{BE}}(\h,\Bbbk)
the corresponding Elias-Williamson diagrammatic category.
We define an element
x\in W
to be
p
-tight
if
{}^{p}b_{x}=b_{\ux}
for some reduced expression
\ux
of
x
.

Remark 10.1
.

An element
x\in W
is called tight if
b_{x}=b_{\ux}
for some reduced expression
\ux
of
x
.
These have been studied extensively in
[
Deo2
,
Lus
,
BW
,
BJ
]
.
When
\Bbbk
is a field of characteristic 0,
p
-tightness reduces to the classical notion of tightness.
Note that unlike tightness,
p
-tightness depends on the realisation rather than the Coxeter system.

When
W
is the Weyl group of a reductive algebraic group
G
, tight elements have a natural geometric interpretation.
An element
x\in W
is tight if and only if there is some reduced expression
\ux
of
x
such that the corresponding Bott-Samelson resolution
\pi_{\ux}:\BS(\ux)\rightarrow X_{x}
of the Schubert variety
X_{x}
is a small resolution
[
BJ
, §2]
.

Consider a stratified variety
Y=\sqcup_{s}Y_{s}
satisfying the conditions of
[
JMW
, §2.1]
so that the category of parity sheaves on
Y
is defined.
Let
\pi:\tilde{Y}_{s}\rightarrow\overline{Y_{s}}
be a resolution of singularities that is even in the sense of
[
JMW
, §2.4]
, then the decomposition theorem for parity sheaves implies
\pi_{*}\underline{\Bbbk}_{\tilde{Y}_{s}}
decomposes as a direct sum of (shifts of) indecomposable parity sheaves.
We say the morphism
\pi:\tilde{Y}_{s}\rightarrow\overline{Y_{s}}
is a
p
-small resolution of singularities
if
\pi_{*}\underline{\Bbbk}_{\tilde{Y}_{s}}
is indecomposable.
If
\Bbbk
is a complete local ring, then
[
JMW
, §2.5]
implies
\pi_{*}\underline{\Bbbk}_{\tilde{Y}_{s}}
remains indecomposable under modular reduction.
In particular, a
p
-small resolution
\pi:\tilde{Y}_{s}\rightarrow\overline{Y_{s}}
depends on
p
rather than the choice of
\Bbbk
.

Remark 10.2
.

If
\Bbbk
is a field of characteristic
0
, then the classical decomposition theorem implies
\pi_{*}\underline{\Bbbk}_{\tilde{Y}_{s}}
decomposes as a direct sum of (shifts of) simple intersection cohomology sheaves.
In particular, if
\pi_{*}\underline{\Bbbk}_{\tilde{Y}_{s}}
is indecomposable then
\pi:\tilde{Y}_{s}\rightarrow\overline{Y_{s}}
is a small resolution.

Remark 10.3
.

In
[
HoW
, §5]
it is shown that if
Y
is any irreducible variety and
\pi:\tilde{Y}\rightarrow Y
is any resolution of singularities, then
\pi_{*}\underline{\Bbbk}_{\tilde{Y}}
contains a canonical indecomposable direct summand
\cE(Y)
with dense support, which is independent of the choice of resolution.
One could more generally define a resolution
\pi:\tilde{Y}\rightarrow Y
to be
\Bbbk
-small if
\pi_{*}\underline{\Bbbk}_{\tilde{Y}}
is indecomposable.

10.2
Elementary properties of
p
-tight elements and
p
-small resolutions

We now note elementary properties of
p
-tight elements and
p
-small resolutions.

Recall the notions of Soergel bimodules
[
Soe2
]
and their generalisations due to Abe
[
Abe
]
.
For any Soergel bimodule
B
we denote by
\grrk B
its graded rank as an
R
-module.

Proposition 10.4
.

Let
\Bbbk
be a field of characteristic
p
or a complete local ring with residue field of characteristic
p
.
Let
\h
be a realisation over
\Bbbk
of an arbitrary Coxeter system
(W,S)
.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

The element
x\in W
is
p
-tight for the realisation
\h
;

(2)

The indecomposable Soergel bimodule
B_{x}
has graded rank
\grrk B_{x}=(v+v^{-1})^{\ell(x)}
;

(3)

For every reduced expression
\ux
of
x
there is an isomorphism
B_{\ux}\cong B_{x}
in
\DBE(\h)
;

(4)

For every reduced expression
\ux
of
x
we have
{}^{p}h_{\id,x}=\sum_{\ux^{e}_{\bullet}=\id}v^{\text{df}(e)}
.

If
W
is the Weyl group of a reductive algebraic group
G
and
\h
a Cartan realisation, then (1)-(4) are also equivalent to:

(5)

For any reduced expression
\ux
of
x
, the Bott-Samelson resolution
\pi_{\ux}:\BS(\ux)\rightarrow X_{x}
is a
p
-small resolution;

(6)

For any reduced expression
\chi(\cE_{x}|_{\id})=\chi(\pi_{\ux,*}(\underline{\Bbbk}_{\BS(\ux)})|_{\id})
, where
\chi(\mathcal{F}|_{z})
denotes the Euler characteristic of the stalk of
\mathcal{F}
at
zB/B
.

Proof.

(1)\implies(2)
: This is immediate from the fact that
\grrk B_{s}=v+v^{-1}
.

(2)

\implies

(1)
:
Since
B_{x}
occurs as a multiplicity-1 direct summand of
B_{\ux}
for any reduced expression
\ux
of
x
, we have a coefficient-wise inequality
\grrk B_{x}\preceq\grrk B_{\ux}=(v+v^{-1})^{\ell(x)}
.
This inequality is strict if and only if
B_{\ux}
contains a non-trivial direct summand that is not
B_{x}
.

(2)

\implies

(3)
:
Suppose, for a contradiction, there exists some reduced expression
\ux
of
x
such that
B_{\ux}\not\cong B_{x}
.
Then
B_{\ux}
contains a non-trivial direct summand
B
where
B\not\cong B_{x}
.
As
B
is non-trivial we know
\grrk B\neq 0
, which would contradict
(2)
.

(3)

\implies

(1)
: This is clear.

(1)\iff(4)
: Deodhar’s defect formula
[
Deo2
, §4]
states
b_{\ux}=\sum_{y}\sum_{\ux^{e}_{\bullet}=y}v^{\text{df}(e)}\delta_{y}
.
Hence
{}^{p}h_{y,x}=\sum_{\ux^{e}_{\bullet}=y}v^{\text{df}(e)}
if and only if
b_{\ux}={}^{p}b_{x}
.
By
(2)
this is independent of the choice of reduced expression.

(1)\iff(5)
: For any expression
\ux=(s_{1},\dots,s_{k})
we have by
[
RW1
, §10.2]
that
\pi_{\ux,*}\underline{\Bbbk}_{\BS(\ux)}\cong\cE_{s_{1}}\star\dots\star\cE_{s_{k}}
.
Hence
\ch[\pi_{\ux,*}\underline{\Bbbk}_{\BS(\ux)}]=b_{\ux}
.
The equivalence follows immediately.

(4)\iff(6)
: This follows from the parity-vanishing property of parity sheaves and the definition of the character map in Section
4.1
which imply
\chi(\cE_{x}|_{y})=(-1)^{\ell(x)}{}^{p}h_{y,x}(1)
.
∎

Remark 10.5
.

Computing local intersection forms is, in general, extremely computationally demanding;
see
[
GJW
, §4.9]
for a discussion of some issues arising from the implementation of their algorithm.
To naively check
x
is
p
-tight all intersection forms must be computed.
Statements (4) and (6) in Proposition
10.4
reduces this problem to computing
one

p
-Kazhdan-Lusztig polynomial or Euler characteristic, which may be more tractable.

The elements
x\in W
which can be
p
-tight are highly constrained.

Lemma 10.6
.

Suppose
x
is
p
-tight for a realisation
\h
.
If
y<x
then
y
is
p
-tight.

Proof.

Suppose
x
is
p
-tight with reduced expression
\ux
.
Write
\ux
as the concatenation
\ux=\uy\uw\uz
for some expressions
\uy,\uw,\uz
.
Then
B_{\uy}
,
B_{\uw}
, and
B_{\uz}
must each be
p
-tight, as any non-trivial summand in
B_{\uy}
,
B_{\uw}
, or
B_{\uz}
would give rise to a non-trivial summand in
B_{\ux}\cong B_{\uy}B_{\uw}B_{\uz}
.
The claim follows from the case when
\uw=s
.
∎

Proposition 10.7
.

If
x
is
p
-tight for some realisation
\h
and prime
p
, then
x
is fully commutative.

Proof.

Suppose
x
is
p
-tight, but not fully-commutative.
Then some reduced expression
\ux
can be expressed as the concatenation
\uy\uw_{I}\uz
where
\uw_{I}
is a reduced expression for the longest word in a Coxeter system
(W_{I},I)
of type
\textbf{I}_{2}(m)
where
m>2
.
But
B_{\uw_{I}}
is never
p
-tight when
m>2
, as the indecomposable Soergel (or Abe) bimodule
B_{w_{I}}\cong R\otimes_{R^{I}}R
has (ungraded) rank
|W_{I}|<2^{\ell(w_{I})}
, which contradicts the argument in Lemma
10.6
.
∎

Remark 10.8
.

The proposition implies, for a fixed realisation of any Coxeter system
W
, a sequence of inclusions:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{tight}\\
\text{elements}\end{array}\right\}\subseteq\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{$p$-tight}\\
\text{elements}\end{array}\right\}\subseteq\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{fully commutative}\\
\text{elements}\end{array}\right\}.

Remark 10.9
.

If
W_{pt}
denotes the
p
-tight elements of
W
, the proof of Lemma
10.6
shows
\langle B_{x}~|~x\notin W_{pt}\rangle_{\oplus,(1)}
forms a tensor ideal in
\sD_{\text{BE}}(\h,\Bbbk)
.

We conclude by noting some examples of
p
-tightness in Cartan realisations of classical groups.

Example 10.10

(The Hexagon permuation)
.

Let
(W,S)
be type
\tA_{7}
with the usual ordering of simple reflections.
Define

\displaystyle\underline{\text{hex}}=(s_{5},s_{6},s_{7},s_{3},s_{4},s_{5},s_{6},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4},s_{5},s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}),

\displaystyle\uw=(s_{2},s_{3},s_{2},s_{5},s_{6},s_{5}).

Both
\underline{\text{hex}}
and
\uw
are reduced expressions; we denote by
hex
and
w
their respective images in
W
.
One easily checks
b_{\underline{\text{hex}}}=b_{\text{hex}}+b_{w}
.
Moreover,
hex
is the Bruhat minimal element in type
\tA
that is fully-commutative, but not tight
[
BW
, §1]
.
In
[
WB12
]
it was shown
{}^{2}b_{\text{hex}}=b_{\text{hex}}+b_{w}
and
hex
is the Bruhat minimal element such that
{}^{p}b_{x}\neq b_{x}
for some
p
.
In particular,
hex
is 2-tight, and
2
-tightness appears at the first possible opportunity in type
\tA
.

Example 10.11
.

Let
\h
be a Cartan realisation of type
\tG_{2}
, and let
s_{0}
be the simple reflection corresponding to the short root.
Then
{}^{3}b_{s_{0}s_{1}s_{0}}=b_{\underline{s_{0}s_{1}s_{0}}}
, so
s_{0}s_{1}s_{0}
is
3
-tight, but neither tight nor
2
-tight.

10.3
2-tightness in type
\tB_{n}

We conjecture that
2
-tight elements are abundant in type
\tB_{n}
.

Conjecture 10.12
.

Let
(W,S)
be a Coxeter system of type
\tB_{n}
,
\h
a Cartan realisation of the same type,
I\subset S
a parabolic of type
\tA_{n-1}
.
Any
x\in W^{I}
, considered as an element of
W
, is
2
-tight.

The validity of Conjecture
10.12
, together with the quotient construction of the antispherical category in Section
4.3
, would imply a geometric proof of the characteristic-2 phenomena in Theorem
1.1
.
However, it is not clear from this Conjecture that every indecomposable parity sheaf in
\Parity_{Wh,I}(\check{B}\backslash\check{G})
is perverse.

Remark 10.13
.

The conjecture has been checked for all elements
x\in W^{I}\subseteq W
up to rank
7
using the ASLoc and IHecke software of Gibson, Jensen, and Williamson
[
GJW
]
.

Remark 10.14
.

By Lemma
10.6
the conjecture holds if and only if
w_{0}w_{I}
is
2
-tight for the Cartan realisation of type
\tB_{n}
.

Acknowledgements and figures

11.1
